2026-07-19 · apps/jobs (wt-jobs) — publishPost + expired-hold reaper + plan-week shipped; the recon corrected more of the brief than it confirmed. (1) **Three premises in the task spec were wrong, and reading the source was the only way to know**: `formatForPlatform` is exported from `@sahoda/shared`, NOT `@sahoda/publishing`, and it performs NO validation at all (`validateVariant` is the only real gate, and it must run BEFORE the adapter because the fixture accepts anything); there is NO adapter registry anywhere (`grep` for `adapterFor|getAdapter|createAdapter|ADAPTERS` returns zero) so the channel switch is hand-written; and `post_publish_logs` ALREADY HAS the `mode` column the brief assumed was missing (`content.sql:74`). **When a task description and the schema disagree, the schema wins — but check, because it cut both ways here.** (2) **The reaper's race-safety was already free, and the real hazard was the opposite of the one specified.** `settles_entry_id uuid unique` (`billing_ledger.sql:71`) plus a pre-check under `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` on the balance row (`:139`/`:164`) means a late DEBIT and the sweep's RELEASE serialize and exactly one commits — nothing to invent, only to prove. The undocumented danger is that **the HOLD TTL is 10 min and the spec asked for a 5-min sweep reaping strictly past `hold_expires_at`**: nothing reaps today, so this is latent, and _landing the reaper activates it_. A slow-but-alive run would get its hold reaped mid-flight, its DEBIT would raise `HOLD_ALREADY_SETTLED`, `withCredits` would convert that to `PROVIDER_ERROR` — user shown a failure for work that succeeded and was never charged. Fixed with a 10-min grace margin (owner ruling) AND `maxDuration: 300` in `trigger.config.ts`, which is below the 600s TTL so a run structurally cannot outlive its own hold. **Losing the settlement race is counted as `alreadySettled`, never as a failure** — the DEBIT winning means real work was charged, which is the correct outcome. (3) **`mode` on the log must come from the ADAPTER's result, never the configured mode**, or a fixture publish gets recorded as live the moment someone flips the env var while the token path is still broken; pinned by a test that configures `live`, hands it a fixture adapter, and asserts the log says `fixture`. Same honesty rule made the channel switch THROW `NO_ADAPTER` for linkedin (which `CONSTRAINTS` marks publishable but ships no adapter) rather than silently falling back to the fixture. (4) **BLOCKED, and deliberately not routed around**: `packages/publishing` exports zero vault symbols and its `exports` map has no subpath, so there is no sanctioned producer for `PublishRequest.auth.accessToken` — a live publish is unimplementable from apps/jobs. The workaround (read `TOKEN_VAULT_KEY`, re-implement AES-256-GCM with `node:crypto`) was available and NOT taken: it duplicates a deliberately private envelope format. Instead `createConnectionResolver({ loadConnection, openSecret })` leaves `openSecret` unwired, fails with `TOKEN_VAULT_UNAVAILABLE`, and still writes an honest `failed` log row. Filed in `apps/jobs/REQUESTS.md`; when the opener lands, one argument changes. (5) **Keep the job core free of the scheduler SDK.** Every core (`runPublishPost`, `sweepExpiredHolds`, `runPlanWeekJob`) is unit-tested with zero Trigger.dev involvement; only the three files under `src/trigger/` import it. This is not tidiness — Trigger.dev has never been deployed from this repo, every workspace package ships raw TS with no build step under `moduleResolution: "Bundler"`, and that combination is untested against its bundler, so the fallback `apps/jobs/CLAUDE.md` already sanctions (Vercel cron + QStash) has to stay a wrapper swap. (6) **`planWeek` runs with `maxAttempts: 1` on purpose**: it charges credits and mints a fresh server-derived `objectRef` per invocation, so a framework retry would mint a NEW ref and take a SECOND real charge — retry is the user's decision, not the runner's. Conversely `publishPost` gets x3 exponential _because_ adapters classify and never retry. (7) An integration test against the LIVE schema is the only thing that catches column drift — a fake client happily accepts a column that doesn't exist; 9 such tests pin `post_publish_logs`' append-only behaviour (one row per attempt, since `block_mutations` is NOT bypassed by service_role, so mutable state must live on `post_variants`). (8) Flake seen once and NOT reproduced: `packages/db`'s `bootstrap.test.ts` failed during a parallel `turbo test` and then passed both in isolation and under a deliberate concurrent re-run of both live-DB suites (105/105 + 55/55). Adding a second live-DB test package raises contention on the shared dev project; if it recurs, suspect connection pressure before logic. wt-jobs
2026-07-19 · packages/billing (wt-billing phase 2) — Cashfree sandbox rail + entitlements gate + carry-forward hardening; 235 green (was 48), 5 live tests opt-in. Decisions: (1) **`period` is `YYYY-MM`, enforced at the GRANT boundary** — it is the sole replay anchor inside `monthlyGrantKey`, so `'2026-7'` and `'2026-07'` were two idempotency keys for one month, i.e. a double-grant. Enforcing only at the providers would have left the hole; `applyPlanGrant` is where the key is actually built. `currentPeriod` derives from **UTC** — an IST server reading local months rolls the period ~5.5h early and mints a second key for a month already granted. (2) **Outer catches return fixed strings.** apps/web renders `credits.error.message` as customer copy (`brand-resolve.ts:174`), so the caller's own `'MESH_ERROR'` sentinel was being shown to users, and a DB failure would have shown a connection string. Debuggability was preserved rather than traded away via an optional `onError(cause, traceId)`; a throwing hook is swallowed so it cannot turn a typed error into a rejection. No existing test asserted the leaky messages — that is why it reached prod. (3) **Entitlements gate (ruling #5)** takes `workspaceId` explicitly and reads no request-scoped context, because "every AI entry point" includes apps/jobs (Trigger.dev — no Clerk `auth()`, no cookies); a `cookies()`-based gate simply would not run there. Returns a `Result`, never rejects, matching `withCredits` — a throwing gate gets swallowed by the call site's outer catch. `loopLevel` is a **level, not a count**: it allows `requested == limit` while countable dimensions deny at `usage >= limit`; conflating them either blocks a paid tier or grants one above it. Limits come from `PLAN_CATALOG` (the DB `plans.limits` is its seeded fold and typed as loose `JsonbSchema`), made honest by a new real-DB **drift test** asserting the table equals the catalog — nothing asserted that before, so DB and code could drift silently. (4) **Cashfree**: signature is `base64(HMAC_SHA256(secret, x-webhook-timestamp + rawBody))`, timestamp first, no separator — this REPLACED a pre-2022 `ksort` scheme that most online snippets still show. Tests recompute the expectation from the spec rather than pasting a constant. Verify over the **raw** body: a re-stringified parse normalizes `1.80` → `1.8` and breaks the HMAC. Added a ±5min freshness window because Cashfree does **no** replay protection and a captured signature stays valid forever. A missing timestamp is a rejection, never an empty string (that would verify a body-only HMAC). (5) **No documented Cashfree hosted-checkout URL exists** — Create Order returns only a `payment_session_id` for the browser SDK, and the widely-copied `POST /pg/view/sessions/checkout` pattern appears in no official docs. Payment Links _do_ return a real `link_url` but emit `PAYMENT_LINK_EVENT` with no `cf_payment_id`, no `order_tags`, and a Cashfree-generated `order_id` — a rewrite of the webhook pipeline for a URL. Chose Orders + an app-owned bridge route + a new `CheckoutSession.sessionId`. (6) **`order_tags` echo into the webhook is confirmed by Cashfree's SDK types but by NO published example** (every sample shows `order_tags: null`); `GET /orders/{id}` returning tags IS documented with examples — so `resolveWebhookEvent` ships a fallback rather than trusting the assumption. (7) **`CASHFREE_ENV` is required and never defaulted**: sandbox and production credentials are structurally identical with no distinguishing prefix, so nothing downstream can catch a prod key aimed at the wrong host; `baseUrl` is derived from it so the two cannot disagree. (8) **BLOCKER — `.env` has `CASHFREE_APP_ID` == `CASHFREE_SECRET_KEY`** (byte-identical, verified without printing either), so live sandbox returns 401 and the tag echo stays unverified; live tests gated behind `CASHFREE_LIVE=1` so a broken credential does not redden the default suite. Filed in `packages/billing/REQUESTS.md` §4.
2026-07-19 · packages/billing (wt-billing phase-2 review) — 4-lens adversarial review with 2 independent skeptics per finding: 20 raised, 18 refuted, **2 survived** (both unanimous). Fixed: (a) neither pg Pool registered an `'error'` listener — node-postgres emits `'error'` on the Pool when an IDLE client fails (pooler idle timeout, failover, restart) and Pool is an EventEmitter, so with no listener Node treats it as an uncaught exception and kills the process; a long-lived server holding a module-level singleton dies on a routine connection recycle. Skeptics reproduced it against the pinned pg-pool by driving a fake idle client, and correctly downgraded CRITICAL→HIGH (availability, not data integrity — an interrupted HOLD is already covered by attempt-derivation). (b) `raw` was the zod-PARSED payload; `WebhookSchema` is non-strict, so the `billing_webhook_events` audit row silently lost `bank_reference` / `payment_time` / `payment_method` / `customer_details` — exactly what reconciles a disputed charge. Caught because the tags-missing fallback path already preserved the delivered body while the happy path did not; the two disagreed, which is what exposed it. **Review lessons:** (1) _verify disputed findings yourself_ — the `?host=` DSN item was refuted, but running the real `pg-connection-string` parser showed `new URL().hostname` and pg's target genuinely disagree, so the mechanism was real; the skeptics were nonetheless right that it is **not** exploitable (anyone who can write the DSN gets a strictly stronger attack by putting the evil host in the authority, where `pgSsl` returns `undefined` = no TLS at all). Fixed as defence-in-depth and the commit message corrected to stop overclaiming — a check that disagrees with the behaviour it guards is worth fixing on its own terms. (2) Two findings were refuted primarily on _"no caller exists yet"_ (fixture-mode grants, replayed-grant on a genuine second payment); that is a correct verdict for this package but converts into an obligation on whoever mounts the route, so both were filed in REQUESTS.md rather than closed. (3) A test asserting a leak is absent can itself be vacuous — the `expect.objectContaining({message: expect.not.stringContaining(secret)})` form was verified to actually FAIL on a planted leak before being trusted.
2026-07-20 · packages/billing (wt-billing merge prep) — merge-readiness review after rebase, then fixed what it confirmed; 247 green (was 235). Decisions: (1) **A failed payment is not a failed webhook.** `processPaymentEvent` drove EVERY event into `applyPlanGrant`, which handles only `payment_succeeded` — so Cashfree's routine `PAYMENT_FAILED_WEBHOOK`/`PAYMENT_USER_DROPPED_WEBHOOK` returned `!ok` (non-2xx → provider redelivers), landed a `failed` audit row, and `pgStore` re-drove it because only `'processed'` is terminal: an infinite redelivery loop whose audit trail recorded a failure where nothing failed. Added a third `ProcessResult.status`, `ignored` — the DTO distinguishes it while the row is marked `processed`, because `billing_webhook_events.status` is constrained to ('received','processed','failed') and calling it `failed` would be the same lie; a fourth status needs a wt-db migration this package must not write. (2) **The defect hid because the test double could only express the happy path.** `FixtureProvider` exposed only `emitPaidEvent`, so no integration test could produce a non-succeeded delivery — 235 tests were green over an unreachable branch. Added `emitEvent({eventType})`; a double that cannot express failure hides exactly the bugs that live there. Verified by reverting the fix and watching them go red — precisely: 4 of the 9 new tests discriminate (2 unit + 2 integration), and the adversarial pass caught the first draft of this very line overclaiming that all of them did. The remaining 5 pin audit fidelity and duplicate-branch precedence, which is legitimate coverage but is NOT regression-proof of this defect; one of them discarded the Result of the `payment_failed` call and so discriminated nothing. (3) **`?host=` may REPEAT and pg keeps the LAST.** The earlier fix read `searchParams.get('host')` (the FIRST), so `?host=db.abc.supabase.co&host=evil.com` relaxed TLS verification for Supabase while pg dialled evil.com — the same MITM the fix closed, moved one parameter right. Probed against pg-connection-string 2.14.0: last wins, and a trailing EMPTY value falls back to the authority host, not to the earlier non-empty one. Both rules now pinned. (4) **The entitlements gate is check-then-act and nothing said so.** It compares caller-supplied `currentUsage` under no lock, so two concurrent creates on a 3-site plan both read 2 and both insert. Unfixable inside billing — the gate has no transaction to join and only the caller knows the pending write — so it is documented loudly at the call site and filed as REQUESTS §8. Contrast `apply_ledger_entry`, atomic because check and mutation are one statement: that is why credits get the guarantee and limits do not. (5) **Comments that promise a backstop must be re-checked, not trusted.** Three `withCredits` comments cited a TTL auto-release that did not exist when phase 2 shipped; the real reaper has since landed on main from the jobs lane (b38d81f/471a47c — `holdSweepTask`, every 5 min, releasing via `apply_ledger_entry` with `expiredReleaseKey`, which is why grepping for an `EXPIRE` entry_type missed it; it was attributed to wt-mesh when this work started and git says otherwise). Verified before rewriting the comments to describe it, including the grace margin that stops a slow-but-alive run being reaped mid-flight. (6) **Cashfree live verification is PARKED by owner ruling** — `.env` still has `APP_ID == SECRET_KEY` (byte-identical, re-confirmed against the live API: 401 on 4 of the 5 opt-in live tests; the 5th is a negative test that passes vacuously when every request is rejected). The adapter merges as unverified code behind `PaymentProvider` with checkout on the fixture rail and no route mounting it, so it is inert rather than live; REQUESTS §4 now states exactly which claims are unproven (chiefly the `order_tags` echo).
2026-07-20 · repo (team bug-fix prep) — Claude Code on the web sandboxes clone the repo with NO `.env`, and the Stop-hook gate (`turbo typecheck test`) runs there on every session end — verified in a fresh credential-free clone: all 7 packages pass (14/14 tasks, exit 0) because EVERY live-DB/live-provider suite gates itself with `describe.skipIf`/`runIf` on env presence (db 105/105 skipped, billing 31, jobs 16) and every test script carries `--passWithNoTests`. That guarantee is a CONVENTION, not a mechanism: one new integration test that asserts without a skipIf guard — or creates its client at module top level, which throws before skipIf applies — hard-fails every teammate cloud session at the Stop gate, where a non-technical user cannot recover. New live tests MUST follow the `packages/db/tests/helpers/env.ts` pattern (env-presence flag + lazy client + `describe.skipIf`). The Stop hook itself was deliberately left unchanged: it degrades gracefully today, and weakening it (e.g. skipping when creds are absent) would also skip the unit tests that DO protect teammate PRs.

2026-07-20 · apps/web (wt-obs, Sentry) — wiring an error reporter is the easy half; the review round found more than the build round. (1) **A scrubber is a DoS surface.** The email rule's unanchored `[A-Za-z0-9._%+-]+@` was O(n²): a 200KB base64 blob in an error message stalled the event loop **22 seconds**, inside `beforeSend`, on the crash path, in Node _and_ the user's tab. It was reachable because `@sentry/core` destructures `maxValueLength` with NO default and truncates only `if (maxValueLength)` — unset means `exception.value` is never truncated before your hook sees it. Fix is three layers: bound the quantifier, cap input length, and set `maxValueLength`. Measure the fix in real milliseconds; "linear by construction" was written in a comment and was false. (2) **`\b` at the start of a param-name alternation is a leak.** `\b(token|code|...)=` never matches `oauth_token=`, `__clerk_ticket=`, `invitation_token=` — a word-char prefix defeats the boundary. Match a name _suffix_ instead. A live Clerk SSO ticket in a URL was one thrown render away from Sentry. (3) **Value-shape redaction can't see JSON.** `{"password":"..."}` and opaque provider tokens (`ya29.`, `xoxb-`) match no shape rule; only a **key-name** pass catches them. But key-name matching is substring matching: `auth` eats `author`, and the naive `auth(?!or)` silently stops redacting `Authorization`. Test both directions — the names that must redact AND the names that must survive. (4) **`onRequestError` cannot fire on a caught error.** All 19 server actions swallowed into a `Result` envelope, so the app's entire mutation path reported nothing while looking fully wired. (5) **The adapter was tested; its attachment was not.** Deleting the options builder from `sentry.server.config.ts` killed all scrubbing and left ~977 tests, typecheck and the production build green. Assert the options object that `Sentry.init` actually received, and prove the test by mutation — a test you haven't seen fail is not evidence. (6) **`flush()` ≠ arrival.** It's `Promise.allSettled`, so a 403 from a revoked key drains identically to a stored event, and an unconfigured process returns `true` in 0ms. Report `configured` and `flushed` as separate honest signals rather than one `sent` boolean that lies.

2026-07-25 · repo (deploy pipeline) — production had been unreachable and BOTH suspected causes were wrong; the Vercel build log named the real one in its first line. (1) **A 3-way text merge of `pnpm-lock.yaml` corrupts it silently and git reports success.** Merging main into wt-web (1629ef1, via GitHub's web UI) raised no conflict yet damaged the lockfile TWICE: it duplicated the `snapshots:` key `'@rollup/rollup-darwin-arm64@4.62.2'` (a YAML parse error) AND dropped the entry for `@trigger.dev/sdk@4.5.4(...)`. `merge=union` was NOT the cause and was correctly scoped to LEARNINGS.md the whole time (`git check-attr merge -- pnpm-lock.yaml` → unspecified); ordinary text merge is enough to do this. Guarded with `pnpm-lock.yaml -merge` so git refuses to auto-merge it. (2) **Fixing the parse error was not fixing the lockfile.** Deleting the duplicate key made the YAML valid and `--frozen-lockfile` STILL failed on the missing dependency — pnpm itself printed 'probably caused by a badly resolved merge conflict'. Only a real regeneration repaired both. Cost: `@trigger.dev/sdk` re-resolved 4.5.4 → 4.5.7 inside its declared `^4.5.4`; contained because `apps/jobs` is not in the `@sahoda/web` build graph, checked with `turbo build --dry=json` rather than assumed. (3) **`ERR_PNPM_META_FETCH_FAIL`/`ERR_INVALID_THIS` was a SYMPTOM.** A broken lockfile makes pnpm fall back to full registry re-resolution, and that storm of metadata GETs is what exited 1. The last good build proved it: it logged 'Verifying lockfile against supply-chain policies (489 entries)' and never hit the registry. Chasing the loudest error would have started a pnpm/Node version hunt that fixed nothing. (4) **The author firewall was real but ALREADY SOLVED — deployment history showed it.** `a5f32c3` (authored IDIVASM) was BLOCKED; the identical tree re-authored as SAHODALABS (`24e46d0`) went READY, as did `928f891`. Vercel was serving 928f891 — the newest GOOD build, not a stale one; the real gap was that the commit after it errored. Latent trap found on the way: this worktree's `git config user.name` was the literal placeholder `YOUR_ORIGINAL_GITHUB_USERNAME <your_original_email@example.com>` and had already authored an unpushed commit, which would have been BLOCKED on push. (5) **Verify a duplicate-key claim with a parser that REJECTS duplicates.** `yaml.safe_load` silently keeps the last of a duplicated key, so it pronounced the corrupt lockfile clean and nearly closed the investigation on a false negative; `grep | uniq -d` is equally useless here because every package legitimately appears in both `packages:` and `snapshots:`. Only a strict loader localized it to one key. (6) **`pnpm install` will NOT regenerate a deleted lockfile when node_modules is complete** — it prints 'Already up to date' and writes nothing, even with `--force` or `--lockfile-only`, because it trusts its cached `node_modules/.pnpm/lock.yaml`. That cache is also the recovery path: it is a full valid v9 lockfile. Deleting the lockfile is therefore NOT a reliable way to rebuild it. (7) Local `turbo test` red is not this repo's fault: this box has no default IPv6 route and `.env`'s `SUPABASE_DB_URL` is the IPv6-only DIRECT host, so 7 live-DB files fail `ENETUNREACH` while every non-live test passes. Proven by re-running with the var emptied (guard engages → all green) rather than by editing `.env`; the ap-south-1 pooler URL that fixed this on Vercel never landed locally.

2026-07-25 · apps/jobs (wt-pub, scheduled-publish dispatcher) — shipped dark behind `SAHODA_PUBLISH_DISPATCH_MODE=off|report|on`. (1) **Reading the code found the rules; running the query found the bug.** The classifier was complete, tested and green before the candidate SQL was ever executed against production. Executing it (read-only, SELECT only) surfaced instagram variants sitting `pending` — and instagram is `publishable: false` in CONSTRAINTS. The classifier treated them exactly like a pending X variant, so it would have enqueued a guaranteed `CHANNEL_NOT_PUBLISHABLE`, marked the variant failed, and thereby made every post carrying an instagram variant a permanent partial — held forever, never terminal. Twenty-four passing tests said nothing about it, because the fixtures only ever used publishable channels. **A test suite can only be as imaginative as its fixtures; production data is the only source of shapes you did not think of.** (2) This was the `skipped` bug's identical twin: `skipped` was handled, its structural sibling walked straight through. Same lesson as the earlier sibling-shape guard defect — when you special-case one input shape, enumerate the others reaching the same branch. (3) **A safe default is not automatically the safe behaviour for an invalid value.** `SAHODA_PUBLISH_DISPATCH_MODE` refuses to start on a set-but-unparseable value rather than falling back to `off`: `"ON"`, `"true"` and `""` are all things a half-applied deploy leaves behind, and each means somebody tried to say something. Silently absorbing them into `off` hides a live-publish intent exactly as readily as it hides a typo, and the operator cannot tell which they got. (4) **Guard the write, not just the read.** Ruling: never expire a post with a published variant. Ordering fan-in before expiry narrows the window but cannot close it — the sweep classifies rows it read earlier, and a variant can publish in between. The `NOT EXISTS` clause therefore lives in the UPDATE statement permanently, and both writers return `boolean` so a guard that declines is reported as `blockedByGuard` rather than counted as work done; a sweep reporting work it did not do is worse than one that did nothing. `EXPLAIN` (which executes nothing) confirmed it compiles to a Nested Loop Anti Join over existing indexes. (5) **Mutation-verify the safety property, not just the feature.** Three deliberate mutations — dropping the published-guard, `<` for `<=` on the grace boundary, treating an unknown publish mode as live — each failed the expected test. Without that pass, "24 green" was only evidence the tests ran. (6) A dispatched post is deliberately NOT moved to `publishing`: that status sits outside the gate, so a run that died would strand it with nothing left to rescue it. An idempotent re-dispatch is the better failure mode, which the `${postId}:${channel}:${scheduledAt}` key already buys. (7) Local `turbo test` failed once with 40 files red, then passed 13/13 on an immediate re-run with no edit in between; the failing run itself reported `1330 passed` and named no failing test. Parallel-load flake, not a regression — re-run before investigating.

2026-07-26 · apps/jobs + apps/web (wt-pub, Vercel cron for both sweeps) — the dispatcher and the hold reaper had never run anywhere, and finding out why was most of the work. (1) **"Scheduled" was a string in a source file, not a running job.** `dispatchSweepTask` declared `cron: '*/5 * * * *'`, but a Trigger.dev schedule only exists once the task is DEPLOYED to their cloud, and nothing deploys it: no `.github/workflows` at all, no `deploy` script, no Trigger.dev CLI installed, and `trigger.config.ts` reads `TRIGGER_PROJECT_REF` while `turbo.json` allowlists `TRIGGER_PROJECT_ID`. `holdSweepTask` was in the same state, which means stranded credit HOLDs had no reaper the whole time. Grep for the thing that INVOKES a schedule, not for the schedule. (2) **The idempotency key protected nothing outside Trigger.dev.** `publishIdempotencyKey` is handed to `.trigger()` and used nowhere else; no UPDATE in the repo sets `publish_status = 'publishing'` (the classifier READS it and nobody writes it), and `post_publish_logs` has only a PK. So duplicate-suppression was entirely the queue's, and Vercel documents that cron delivery can both duplicate and miss invocations — publishing from a cron route would have double-posted. Shipped with `enqueuePublish` throwing `PublishQueueUnavailableError`, counted as `queueUnavailable` rather than `failed`, because a correct refusal and a broken thing must not share a number. (3) **The premise about the timeout was three years out of date.** Vercel functions with Fluid compute are 300s default AND maximum on Hobby, 800s max on Pro — the same budget as `maxDuration: 300`, not "well below" it. Read the current limits page rather than reasoning from the 10s/60s numbers everyone remembers. Hobby crons, on the other hand, are capped at ONCE PER DAY and a `*/5` expression fails at DEPLOY time, so the plan genuinely gated on a Pro upgrade. (4) **Clerk would have eaten every tick silently.** The middleware is inverted-deny with `/(api|trpc)(.*)` in its matcher, so an unauthenticated cron GET gets redirected to `/sign-in` — and Vercel cron does not follow redirects, it treats 3xx as final. The job would have logged a green run every five minutes forever while doing nothing. Three files have to agree (vercel.json, the route's location on disk, the middleware allowlist) and every pairing has a silent failure mode, so `wiring.test.ts` reads all three and fails if they drift; verified by deleting the middleware entry. (5) **`timingSafeEqual` throws on unequal lengths.** Comparing raw bearer tokens turns a wrong-length guess into a 500 and leaks the secret's length; comparing fixed-width SHA-256 digests makes every comparison the same shape. Fail closed on an unset secret, too — an endpoint that treats "no secret configured" as "no auth required" is a public mutation endpoint between deploy and configuration. (6) **A module boundary is only real if something checks it.** apps/web must not pull the Trigger.dev SDK, so `sweeps.test.ts` walks the real import graph from the real entry file; it also caught that `runtime.ts` transitively imported `@sahoda/publishing` and `@sahoda/mesh` through its plan-week deps, which would have dragged a model client into a route that only runs SQL. Verified by adding an SDK import three modules down and watching the test name the offending file. (7) The production `next build` is the only proof that a raw-TS workspace package bundles: `@sahoda/shared` is in `transpilePackages` and `@sahoda/billing`/`mesh`/`publishing` never were, so the honest move was to build and look — `/api/cron/sweeps` compiled with no config change.

Built in a dedicated wt-admin worktree because three other Claude sessions were writing apps/web in the primary checkout, and hook-driven QA runs would have attributed their red to these cards. Rulings: (1) **doc 13 §3's predicate is unimplementable as written**: it specifies is_ops_admin() checking ops_admins "by auth.uid()", but auth is Clerk, so auth.uid() is NULL on every request and that policy denies everyone forever — the predicate reads `auth.jwt() ->> 'sub'` (text, a Clerk subject), the same identity app.member_workspace_ids() has used since migration 02. Any doc that says auth.uid() means "the authenticated identity", not that function. (2) **Platform tables get a read policy and NO write policies**: every mutation goes through a public.ops_* SECURITY DEFINER fn that re-checks is_ops_admin() + role, so authorisation lives in one place instead of thirty policy clauses; the RLS test asserts an active OWNER cannot INSERT directly, and that assertion _is_ the write perimeter. (3) Two spec deviations forced by Postgres, not chosen: `column` is a reserved word (→ board_column) and citext installs into the `extensions` schema, which every `set search_path = public` fn here would fail to resolve (→ text + unique index on lower(email)). (4) **Placeholder env values must read as UNSET**: .env carried DEVOPS_INGEST_TOKEN=<the string from 2a>, and accepting it would mean signing ingest requests with a literal angle-bracket string and reporting success — scripts/lib/ops-env.mjs treats <…>/changeme-shaped values as absent and says so on the way out.
2026-07-25 · packages/db (wt-admin) — the ops_changelog author rotation, and a green test that proved the wrong thing. (1) **The sequence must start at 0**: doc 13 §8 fixes `author = authors[seq % 3]` and §17 requires three consecutive entries to read DIVAS → GIRIJA → DIVAS AND GIRIJA. A bigserial starting at 1 satisfies the formula and fails the ordering; `create sequence … start with 0 minvalue 0` satisfies both. Assignment is a BEFORE INSERT trigger so no client can set it; a manual author survives, is flagged author_overridden, and does not shift the cycle because seq is consumed either way. (2) **The first version of the test asserted `after.seq === before.seq + 2`, passed run alone, and failed in the full suite** — vitest runs test files in parallel and ops_rls.test.ts writes a changelog row too. The deeper reason is worse than the race: nextval() is non-transactional, so a rolled-back insert leaves a permanent gap and position in the sequence is never the claim. Rewritten to assert the invariant that _implies_ the ordering — author is a pure function of seq, checked across all three residues. Rule: when an ordered claim rests on a shared sequence, assert the mapping, not the adjacency.
2026-07-25 · packages/shared + apps/web (wt-admin) — grant origins were two bare literals with nothing holding them together. bootstrap_workspace wrote action_type = 'signup_grant' in PL/pgSQL while lib/wallet/entry-copy.ts keyed its copy map off 'signup_grant' in TypeScript; renaming either would have left the producer writing rows and the classifier silently falling through to "Included with your plan" — telling a brand-new user that free credits came with a plan they never bought. GRANT_ORIGIN + adminGrantKey() in @sahoda/shared are now the single source, and packages/db/tests/ledger_key_contract.test.ts reads every migration's p_idempotency_key / p_action_type literals and compares them with what the shared builders emit. Expected prefixes are DERIVED (signupGrantKey('')), never retyped, so the guard cannot drift from the thing it guards; it needs no DB, so it never skips. Mutation-verified rather than assumed: renaming the key prefix fails 3 assertions with an actionable message, renaming an origin fails 1, revert returns to green. **The real defect is one layer down** — credit_ledger.action_type is plain text with no enum and no CHECK, so this is a convention two sides agree to, not something the database enforces. Tracked as SL-032: replace with a grant_origin column or structured meta, backfill, then delete the string matching.
2026-07-25 · apps/web (wt-admin) — how wide the server-component test gap actually is, measured instead of assumed. Server components ARE tested here, by exactly one pattern: `render(await WalletPage())` in (app)/wallet/page.test.tsx — awaiting the async component resolves it to a plain element tree that RTL mounts in jsdom. That covers the component's own async branching and its rendered output, and nothing else. What it cannot see: (a) it is not RSC — only the TOP-level promise is awaited, so a nested async server component is never resolved and multi-level composition goes untested; (b) 'use client' boundaries are not real, everything mounts as one client tree, so a function-valued prop or server-only code pulled into a client component renders fine; (c) server-only is aliased to `export {}` (vitest.server-only-stub.ts), disabling the exact poison pill that enforces "never reaches the client"; (d) middleware is not exercised at all — routing, the /admin 404 and the CSP header are unreachable from vitest, which is why cspFor() and provesOpsAdmin() were extracted as pure predicates with their own tests; (e) notFound()/redirect() throw framework-private errors, so a test can assert the throw but never that a user sees a 404. Everything between "the framework invokes my code" and "the user sees a response" is Playwright's job — and test:smoke is NOT in the Stop hook (turbo run typecheck test only), so that band is unguarded on every commit. Two consequences adopted: keep extracting pure decisions, and /ship must run smoke. If unit-level RSC coverage is ever wanted, react-dom/static's prerender is installed and DOES await nested async components — a node-env *.rsc.test.ts project is the honest route.

2026-07-27 · packages/db (wt-admin, Admin-Ops P3) — **A guard and the thing it guards ship in the same migration.** `ops_admin_half` added fourteen `public.ops_*` functions, three of which reach `app.apply_ledger_entry` by design (maker-checker). The pre-existing guard from SL-016 asserted that NOTHING on the human write path may touch the ledger, and it was registry-driven — so the moment that migration landed, the gate went red and stayed red until a second migration re-categorised the surface. The window between them had no honest green: the only quick fixes were adding `ops_credit_request_verify` to the "must not touch credits" list (which falsifies the assertion for all fifteen at once) or deleting the guard. Both are the fake-success this repo refuses, so the gate simply stayed red across a session boundary and blocked the Stop hook. The premise change belonged in the same commit as the behaviour change. Two shapes worth keeping from the fix: categorise rather than exempt — two lists (`ops_human_write_functions`, `ops_credit_functions`) with a test requiring every function to be in exactly one, so nothing escapes classification and the strict assertion stays strict for everything it still covers; and tie a non-vacuity check to the registry (`rows === registry`) instead of to a literal count, because `expect(rows.length).toBe(5)` went stale at 15 and failed a real guard for a bookkeeping reason.

2026-07-27 · apps/web (wt-admin, Admin-Ops P3) — **A control that the server would refuse is ABSENT, not disabled.** Applied five times across this build and worth stating once: the board's drag/move/archive for a viewer, the code box on your own credit request, the role select and Revoke on the last owner, Approve on an application that already joined, and the embed form's submit with no Turnstile site key. A disabled control still says "this exists for you and is temporarily unavailable"; an absent one says nothing false, and the line that replaces it can say who it IS waiting on. This is only safe because the refusal is enforced somewhere real — `app.ops_writer()`/`app.ops_owner()` raise 42501, the last-owner guard raises, the captcha fails closed — so the UI decision is presentation, never authorisation. Three component tests pin the viewer case, including that no card is draggable without the seat for it.

2026-07-27 · apps/web (wt-admin) — **Verifying a webhook by hand beat adding the dependency.** `@clerk/nextjs/webhooks` imports `svix`, which is not installed, and this repo's lockfile has already cost one production outage. The Svix scheme is fully specified — HMAC-SHA256 over `${id}.${timestamp}.${rawBody}`, secret base64 after its `whsec_` prefix, header carrying space-separated `v1,<base64>` entries — so it is about forty lines. Three things that were only obvious once written: verification must run against the RAW body because re-serialising JSON changes key order and whitespace; the timestamp tolerance must reject the FUTURE as well as the past, or a forgery dated next week passes; and the primary address must be selected by `primary_email_address_id`, because taking `email_addresses[0]` is how an account with two addresses gets bound to the wrong admin seat. All three are mutation-verified — each fails 2-3 tests when reverted.

2026-07-27 · scripts (wt-admin) — **A per-worktree override file goes stale the same way the thing it fixed did.** `ops/.local.env` exists because worktrees share one symlinked `.env` but run separate dev servers, and a sync once posted its board updates at a port belonging to a DIFFERENT worktree, got a clean 404, and the board silently stopped moving. The override fixed that — and then itself named a port nothing was serving, reproducing the identical silent no-op one layer up. The general shape: any file whose whole job is to describe "this checkout, right now" needs something that notices when it stops being true. Until there is one, check `ss -ltn` against the resolved `ingestUrl` before trusting a dead board, because a stale board and a broken UI look the same from the outside.

2026-07-27 · packages/db (wt-admin) — **A parameter on a function granted to `authenticated` is an input from the attacker, not a setting.** `ops_credit_request_verify(uuid, text, boolean)` took `p_allow_self`, and the app read `OPS_ALLOW_SELF_APPROVE` from the environment before passing it. That reads like a server-side gate and is not one: PostgREST exposes the whole signature, so a direct `supabase.rpc()` supplies the boolean itself and the env var never enters into it. Worse, the flag skipped BOTH identity checks, so a third active admin holding any valid code was granted 250 credits — maker-checker reduced to one factor wearing two coats. The fix is to DROP the overload rather than supersede it (Postgres overloads on signature; the exploitable entry point would otherwise stay callable beside its replacement) and move the escape hatch to `current_setting('sahoda.allow_self_approve', true)`, which lives on the database and which a PostgREST caller has no surface to write. General rule: if a client can name it in the call, it is not a gate — it is a request.

2026-07-27 · packages/db (wt-admin) — **Nine passing maker-checker tests proved only that the check works when the caller cooperates.** Every one of them passed `p_allow_self: false`, including the one written specifically to prove the right code in the wrong hands grants nothing. They were honest tests of the honest path, and they were unanimous, and the property they were defending was false the entire time. What found it was writing the test as the ATTACKER rather than as the caller — same RPC, hostile argument. Mutation testing would not have caught this either: mutating the function body breaks the cooperative tests just fine. The gap is in the test INPUTS, not the assertions. For anything that guards an invariant, one test must supply the arguments an adversary would, not the ones the app happens to send.

2026-07-27 · apps/web (wt-admin) — **A comment claiming a test exists is worse than no comment.** `service-rpc.ts`'s header stated that `service-rpc.test.ts` asserted both halves of doc 13 §16's promise — that the module names only allowed RPCs and exports no client. The file did not exist. A reader trusts that sentence and stops looking, so the claim actively removed scrutiny. Writing it revealed the right shape too: it reads the SOURCE, not the exports, because a test that merely calls the three exported functions passes just as happily after someone adds a fourth export that grants credits — it would simply never call it. The question is what the module is CAPABLE of, which is a question about its text. 3/3 mutants killed (smuggled credit RPC, exported client, second module constructing one).

2026-07-27 · packages/db (wt-admin) — **The hardening that broke the feature looked exactly like hardening.** Fixing the `p_allow_self` bypass, I also made `not_the_approver` unconditional — strictly stronger, obviously correct, and wrong. `ops_credit_request_create` already refuses to name the author as approver, so requester and approver are never the same person; check `not_the_approver` first and unconditionally and the self-approval branch behind it becomes unreachable, which silently killed the dev escape hatch doc 13 §6 requires. Nothing became unsafe — every refusal still refused — it just stopped being a control. An EXISTING test caught it by reading a changed `reason` string (`self_approval_blocked` → `not_the_approver`), which is the argument for asserting on the specific reason rather than merely on `ok: false`. The real lesson is about what made the original dangerous: not WHICH checks the flag relaxed, but WHO decided. Once the flag came from a GUC no client can write, relaxing both checks was fine again, and the secure version is the original semantics with a different source of truth.

2026-07-27 · packages/db + apps/web (wt-admin) — **RULE: everything in an RPC signature is attacker-supplied, and every security-relevant function needs at least one test written as the adversary.** PostgREST exposes a function's whole signature to anyone the grant admits, so a parameter is not a setting, a mode, or a hint from the app — it is an input from whoever is calling, and the app's careful value is one of many. The corollary is a testing rule, because the failure that started this was invisible to a full green suite: every existing test called each function the way the APP calls it, with the arguments the app sends. Those tests are necessary and they prove the happy path; they cannot prove a security property, because an attacker does not send the app's arguments. So: for any function that guards an invariant, at least one test must be written from the attacker's chair — hostile arguments, wrong caller, skipped step — and it must assert on the OUTCOME (the balance did not move, the row is still pending) rather than on a returned error string. Mutation testing does not substitute; mutating the body breaks the cooperative tests just fine, so they look well-guarded. The gap lives in the test INPUTS. The sweep this rule came from found three more of the same shape in one pass: a deny path that let any writer take the approver's decision, an import that took the author's name from the payload, and a caller-chosen OTP lifetime.

2026-07-27 · scripts (wt-admin) — **The commit that CREATES a card must not close it.** Scope-only parsing fixed the false positives coming from body prose, and then the same failure walked back in through the front door: `docs(SL-040): card the eight-step walkthrough` was the commit that created that card, and the hook read the scope as a claim of completion. The board then reported a walkthrough as walked that nobody has walked — the exact drift the board exists to prevent, and invisible because a done card looks like progress. The missing distinction is that a scope says WHICH card and a type says WHETHER it is finished: only `feat` and `fix` complete, because carding something (`chore`), describing it (`docs`), writing its tests (`test`) or tidying it (`refactor`) are all real work on a card that is by definition not yet done. Deliberately asymmetric — a missed auto-close costs one `ops-card task X done`, a false close costs someone noticing first. The tell was in the data the whole time: SL-040 was `done` AND `blocked`, a state with no meaning, and a board that can represent a contradiction should be checked for one.
2026-07-19 · packages/db (wt-db) — **INCIDENT: mutation-testing a destructive helper against the shared dev DB wiped every tenant row.** Extended the interruption-recovery sweep to `bootstrap`/`brand_memory`/`connections` and extracted it into `tests/helpers/sweep.ts`. Then, to prove its prefix guard had teeth, I disabled the guard (`if (false)`) and re-ran the suite — but the guard's own test called the REAL sweep with the unsafe prefixes `''` and `'%'` through a live service-role client. With the guard off, `''` expands to the LIKE pattern `%`, which matches every row; combined with `.lt('created_at', now-1h)` it deleted every workspace older than an hour and cascaded through `posts`, `sites`, `leads`, `connections`, `brand_memory`, `credit_ledger`/`credit_balances`, `workspace_themes`, `subscriptions`, plus stale `users_profile` rows. Reference data (`plans`, `guide_tours`) survived because it is not workspace-scoped. Lessons, in order of importance: (1) **A test that asserts "this input is rejected before any I/O" must be handed a client that CANNOT do I/O** — the assertion is about the guard, not the database, so a stub that throws on any use keeps full teeth (the sweep reaches for it and throws the wrong error) while making destruction impossible. Verified by re-running the identical mutation against a planted 5h-old canary: test still fails, canary survives. (2) **Mutation testing is itself a destructive operation when the code under test deletes.** The earlier mutation rounds in this session were safe because `upsert_connection` and the RLS policies only ever _refused_ writes — removing a guard there produced extra rows, not missing ones. The moment the mutated function's job is DELETE, "disable the guard and see what breaks" means "disable the guard and watch it delete". Blast-radius-check the mutation before running it, not just the code. (3) A pre-flight row count would have caught it one round earlier: the wipe happened during a mutation round and I only noticed later, from an unrelated `real_ws: 0` in a summary query. Snapshot counts before and after any destructive run. (4) The dev DB is shared and had real (if regenerable) state — two Clerk dev users' workspaces from wt-web's live auth + brand-resolve verification, including their ledger trail. `db push` is gated behind human approval for exactly this reason; a test that deletes deserves the same suspicion as a migration. wt-db

2026-07-28 · apps/web (wt-admin) — **A mutation run that cannot restore is worse than no mutation run.** Mutating five files with `cp` backups, one backup path did not exist, so the `cp` restore silently failed and THREE mutants stayed applied to `gate-history.ts` — carry-forward across a gap, trend counting window width, and first-run-of-day winning. The final "RESTORED" line then printed `4 failed` and I nearly read it as a flaky suite rather than as my own damage still in the tree. Two habits come out of it: the restore must be verified by the same green it claims (a restored file that does not return the suite to its pre-mutation count has not been restored), and for a file git has never seen there IS no `git checkout` safety net, so mutate only committed files or write the backup somewhere you have just proved exists. The mutants themselves were all killed — 1, 4, 3, 8 and 4 tests respectively — which is the only reason the damage was visible at all.

2026-07-28 · packages/db (wt-admin) — **Check the network before diagnosing the code.** Ten live suites failed at once with `ENETUNREACH` on an IPv6 address, and I wrote most of a diagnosis about the direct-vs-pooler Supabase host before noticing that the SAME suites had passed an hour earlier and that only the pg-pool (direct Postgres) tests were failing while every PostgREST test passed. The machine had moved onto a hotspot with no IPv6 route; twenty minutes later the route came back and all 258 passed untouched. The tell was there in the split — one transport failing and another succeeding against the same project is an environment fact, not a code fact — and the cheap check (`ip -6 route` plus a socket connect) costs seconds against a diagnosis that would have sent someone editing .env. A test failure is evidence about the whole system, and the network is part of the system.

2026-07-28 · apps/web (wt-admin) — **A chart's blind spot belongs ON the chart.** Cumulative flow is reconstructed from `ops_tasks` timestamps because there is no history table, and `ops_task_move` clears a stamp the new column no longer justifies (SL-036) — so a card that reached Done and moved back leaves no trace of the days it spent there. The series therefore under-reports progress. That is the safe direction, but only if the reader knows: an unlabelled chart is read as a measurement rather than a reconstruction. The same principle drove every other decision here — the window stated in words, coverage stated as a sentence, a suite that never ran reading "never ran" instead of green, and a day with no run rendered as a HOLLOW CELL so there is no line to accidentally join across. Drawing a green line through a week nobody tested is the one thing a health chart must never do, and the way to guarantee it is to pick a form that has no line to draw.

2026-08-08 · apps/web (wt-inbox) — **A shared port built against an absent implementation can be worse than no port.** wt-mesh wrote `packages/shared/src/inbox/reads.ts` because the Zernio read client was frozen on a branch it could not merge, and declared its own `declare const profileBrand: unique symbol` to carry the tenant-scoping guarantee across the boundary. That brand is structurally DISTINCT from the one in `packages/publishing/src/zernio/scope.ts`, so an id minted by the real `scopeProfile` is not assignable to the port's `ScopedProfileId` — satisfying both would have forced a cast at the binding file, and the cast IS the tenant boundary failing. The port's own doc-comment named the hazard ("that file should re-export these two types rather than redeclare them") and shipped it anyway, because on a branch where the implementation is absent nothing can prove the two brands disagree. The lesson is not "don't write ports": it is that a port duplicating a NOMINAL type cannot be validated until the implementation is in the same tree, so the duplication has to be the first thing re-examined at reconcile, not the last. The pure modules ported cleanly; only the one that restated a type the other package owns had to be thrown away.

2026-08-10 · packages/shared + packages/db (wt-web) — **Count the copies before you call it a duplicate.** REQUESTS.md logged `savePost`'s terminal-status list as a hand copy of `reschedule_post` — two copies of one rule. It was three: `release_post_for_publish` carries the identical four statuses under a different error name (POST_NOT_RELEASABLE, not POST_NOT_RESCHEDULABLE), so a grep for the ERROR name finds one guard and a grep for the LIST finds two. A fourth copy sat in `posts-save.test.ts`'s `test.each` — the worst place for one, because a test holding its own copy keeps passing against the list it agrees with while the product uses a different one. The fix that survives is the one that can go red: the shared constant alone would have left the SQL free to drift, so `schedule_guard_parity.test.ts` parses the guard back out of both migrations, and it was proven red in both directions (mutate the TS → both fail; mutate one migration → that one fails) before being called done. A parity test that has never been seen to fail is a claim, not a check.
2026-08-10 · apps/web + packages/publishing (wt-metrics) — **A fixture of someone else’s API can only encode what you already believe it returns.** The analytics surface shipped green — 2,318 tests, hand-written fixtures — and was wrong in four ways that no passing test could have caught, because every one of those tests was written from the same assumptions as the code. The load-bearing one: `classifyPostMetrics` rule 6 held that `analytics.lastUpdated` was “the only proof a measurement happened”. Pointed at the live API for the first time, four posts published 17h, 41m, 36m and 26m earlier all came back carrying the IDENTICAL stamp `2026-08-10 09:38:57` — it is a poll stamp, not a measurement stamp. So three of four real posts rendered “Impressions 0 · Reach 0 · Engagement 0” as though measured, through the very field trusted to prevent exactly that. The account half failed the same way twice over: tiles read `reach` as a flat number when it arrives as `{total: 1}` and asked for two keys the endpoint does not have, so all four silently vanished; and `seriesFrom` fell through to `Object.entries({total: 1})` and drew a one-point follower chart dated `"total"` — a JSON key rendered as a date, which is worse than an empty chart because it is a confident sentence about data that does not exist. What generalises is not “test against live” — it is that a fixture is a claim about a system, and when that system is one you do not control, the claim needs a source. The payloads are now recorded under `fixtures/zernio/` with a README saying re-capture, never edit: the moment someone edits one to make a test pass, it goes back to being a statement of what we assume. Four posts are kept rather than one because no single file can show that the stamp is identical across all of them, which is the whole finding.
2026-08-10 · apps/web + packages/publishing (wt-inbox3) — **A `[DOC]` assumption with a green test suite is worse than an untested one.** The inbox measured every reply window from `direction === 'inbound'` — doc 13's word, never observed. Zernio sends `'incoming'`. The suite was green because `messages.test.ts` built its fixtures with `direction: 'inbound'` too: fixture and implementation shared the assumption, so the tests certified the defect and a comment where the failure should have been said so out loud ("if Zernio sends something else, every window degrades to unknown"). Two consequences shipped. The known one was honest — every affordance read `unknown`, which claims nothing but looks exactly like a working feature. The unforeseen one was not: `message-list.tsx` compared the SAME literal to pick which side a bubble renders on, with an explicit "anything not inbound is ours" fallback, so every message including the customer's rendered in the owner's colour labelled **"You"** — Sahoda putting the customer's words in the shop owner's mouth, in the thread they were reading to decide how to reply. One wrong string, two independent bugs, and grepping the literal was the only thing that found the second. Three rules came out of it: a fixture must be captured, not authored alongside the code it tests (all six now live in `packages/publishing/fixtures/zernio-inbox/`); a wire vocabulary gets exactly one interpreter (`messageDirection`), because a second comparison is a second bug nobody will connect to the first; and an unrecognised value must resolve to `unknown` AND log, never default to a side — Instagram is one platform of three with a modelled window, and "wrong until the first Facebook thread" is a different outcome from "wrong forever". The same probe corrected three more claims the code stated confidently: `meta` does not ride on every `/inbox/*` response, `/inbox/comments/{postId}` omits `nextCursor` outright, and `/inbox/comments` returns EVERY post — so `rows > 0` was permanently true and "No comments yet" was unreachable on the surface named for it. Worth noting what did NOT break: the `accountId` join everyone feared, flagged in three places as the thing that would 404 every row while the list rendered perfectly, was correct. The dangerous unknown and the actual defects were different unknowns, which is the argument for calling the API rather than reasoning about it.
2026-08-11 · apps/web + packages/publishing (wt-analytics2) — **An optional parameter with a plausible default is an omission nobody sees.** `classifyPostMetrics` accepted `lagHours?: number` defaulting to Instagram's 48, and `listPostMetrics` — the caller that classifies every channel of every post in the app — never passed one. Every X, GBP and LinkedIn post was therefore judged against Instagram's reporting window, silently, and it stopped being theoretical on 2026-08-10 when a LinkedIn post published on the live account. The lesson was already in the same file: `simulated` shipped optional a day earlier, one of two call sites omitted it, and the comment above it says "Required makes the omission a compile error instead". The same fix applied twice in one file within two days is a signal about the shape, not the field. What made this more than a re-run: the OBVIOUS repair is worse than the bug. `lagHours` does not only decide the date shown — it is the gate on rule 6a, which fires only on `reason === 'lag'`, so a per-channel number table with `linkedin: 0` stops the demotion and lets an all-zero LinkedIn payload fall through to `ready`, rendering "0 impressions" under a poll stamp. That trades a false promise for a fabricated zero, and only one of those is a claim about the customer's business. Nothing in the repo states a post-level window for the other three channels — the post payload carries no `dataDelay` at all, on any of eight live posts, though the account endpoints do — so the ignorance is modelled (`{ known: false }`) rather than filled in, and an unknown window can never license a zero at any age. The discriminating test is the one that fails on BOTH the old code and the plausible repair; the "wrong date" test alone passes the repair that fabricates. Re-capturing the same posts a day later paid for itself twice over: the three held as pending on 08-10 now report real numbers, which is the only available evidence that rule 6a withheld a zero rather than hid data, and the LinkedIn leg carries its own sync stamp 23 minutes off the Instagram batch — sweeps are per platform, so a shared window was wrong in principle and not merely in arithmetic. Finally, the honesty rules do not survive aggregation for free: a `pending` post coalesced to 0 to sort by lands at the bottom of a ranking, which says "reached the fewest people" without ever drawing a zero, and a total that skips it is a subtotal wearing a total's clothes. Every figure now carries the coverage it was computed from, per metric — a `ready` state can still hold `impressions: null`.
2026-08-11 · packages/shared + packages/publishing + apps/web (wt-inbox4) — **A function that is correct in isolation can still be fed the wrong input, and the defence written into it hides that.** `newestInboundAt` scans a page of messages without assuming order, and says so in its doc comment — a deliberate guard against intra-page ordering. It was still wrong, because the guard protects against the order WITHIN a page and the bug was about WHICH page: `GET /inbox/conversations/{id}/messages` defaults to `sortOrder=asc`, oldest-first, so a 50-row read of a long thread returns the fifty OLDEST messages and the newest inbound one — the only timestamp a reply window is measured from — is not in it at all. Instagram and Facebook replay up to 500 messages per conversation on connect, so threads longer than one page are ordinary rather than exotic. While the inbox was read-only this produced a wrong badge, which is why nobody looked: the surface degraded to a plausible `closed` and every test passed, because every fixture was one page long and one page is always its own newest page. Wiring sending turned it into a refusal to reply to a customer who wrote an hour ago. The fix belongs at the fetch (`sortOrder: 'desc'`, with `sortOrderApplied` read back because Facebook and Bluesky ignore the request and only reverse intra-page), NOT inside the scanner — the scanner's logic was never the defect. Two smaller findings came out of the same lane and are worth keeping: three sibling endpoints return three DIFFERENT success envelopes (`data.messageId`, `data.commentId`, and `reply.id` with no `data` and no `success` wrapper), so one shared accessor would have resolved to undefined on reviews and reported every review reply as unconfirmed forever; and the literal-`false` `canSendFromSahoda` left behind by the read lane did exactly what its author intended — widening it was impossible to do by accident, and the diff that widened it is the diff that had to justify itself.
2026-08-11 · repo-wide (wt-web, merging four lanes) — **A green gate can be completely silent about the edit you just made, because nothing hashed it.** Rewriting every board card in `scripts/lib/ops-cards.mjs` and re-running the full gate returned `Tasks: 24 successful, 24 cached >>> FULL TURBO`. Turbo hashes each package's OWN directory, and that file sits at the repo root, so no package's hash moved and nothing re-ran. The two tests that exist to guard exactly that file — `card-copy.test.ts` and `seed-parity.test.ts` — live in `apps/web` and reach OUT of the package to import it by relative path, which is what puts them outside their own package's cache key. Run by name they were fine (14 and 5), but that was luck rather than knowledge: `card-copy.test.ts` fails a card title containing a file path, and a title with one in it would have gone green all the way to production. The rule that generalises is not about turbo — it is that `>>> FULL TURBO` on a tree you have just edited is evidence of a HASHING GAP, never of correctness, so after touching anything under `scripts/`, `ops/state/`, or any root file a test imports by path, run that test by name and quote THAT result instead. Same family as SL-077's `format:check` finding — a true count silent about what it never examined — and NOT fixed by it, because that one was a script outside turbo and this one is a file outside every package.
2026-08-11 · apps/web (wt-web, merging wt-analytics2) — **A route-literal type error naming a route that visibly exists on disk is a stale artifact, not a bug.** The merge failed the gate with `rail.tsx(20,5): error TS2322: Type '"/analytics"' is not assignable to type 'Route'`, while `src/app/(app)/analytics/page.tsx` sat right there in the merged tree. `typedRoutes: true` compiles `Route` from a union generated into `.next/types/routes.d.ts` — a GITIGNORED BUILD ARTIFACT, in this worktree four weeks stale — so typecheck was judging today's routes against last month's build. The lane was green because it had built; the merge was red because this worktree had not. The fix is `pnpm exec next typegen`, not a code change. What makes it dangerous is the error's grammar: it names the NEW route as the invalid thing, so both obvious readings — the lane added a bad nav entry, or the merge dropped the page — are wrong and both lead to DELETING the line that is correct. Two things worth carrying: the gate's typecheck silently depends on a build artifact that no branch's green count can vouch for, and a fresh clone or a CI runner with no `.next/` at all starts in exactly the same position as a stale one, so this is reachable by anyone, not just by someone merging.
2026-08-12 · apps/web (wt-web) — **A finding read off the orchestrator and not the leaf is half a finding, and the half that is wrong is the half that gets acted on.** A pre-walk audit filed "Regenerate costs a second full 50 credits and reads as 'try again'", derived from `onboarding-flow.tsx` — where `handleRegenerate` dispatches the same paid action as Generate, which is true — without opening `brand-card.tsx`, three files down, where every one of the six buttons has read `Regenerate · Uses 50 credits` all along. The cost was never hidden. Had the finding been implemented as filed, the work would have been to add a label that already existed, and the review would have found nothing wrong because the screen would indeed show the cost. What the mis-read concealed is that the defect was one layer over and the opposite shape: Spark refuses the spend it cannot complete (`disabled={insufficient}`, plus an alert naming required against available), and Refine — the screen where the user has ALREADY been charged once and is deciding whether to spend again — degraded the identical failure to `toast(state.message)`, a message that vanishes and carries neither number. Six live buttons kept offering a 50-credit action to a wallet holding nothing, and pressing one was the only way to find out. Two rules come out of it. First, a claim about what a user READS has to be verified at the element that renders the words, never at the component that owns the handler — the handler proves what is charged, not what is said, and those are different files by design here. Second, when two screens invoke the same paid action, the audit question is not "does each show the cost" but "do they refuse it the same way"; the affordance was symmetric and the REFUSAL was not, which is the same one-guarantee-held-per-consumer shape as findings 1-4 in `docs/ux-findings.md`, arriving through the error path instead of the happy one. Both fixes are pinned by mutation rather than by assertion count: reverting the Home branch and the Refine guard fails six named tests, and the Refine file asserts all six buttons disable together precisely because a per-card guard is how this regresses. The companion fix in the same commit has the same origin — Home renders the `unreadable` em dash for a workspace that does not exist, a state `/wallet` had already split correctly, so the product told every brand-new user "—" over "credits to spend" on the first screen it ever shows them.
2026-08-12 · apps/web (wt-brainui) — **A meter that counts filled fields is a lie the moment one model call fills them all.** `signalClarityPercent` counted non-empty leaves across the Brand Brain, so the Refine step read ~100% the instant the 50-credit resolve returned — a full meter certifying that Sahoda had guessed fifteen things about a business it had never heard of. Filled and confirmed are different claims and only one of them is worth measuring; the ring now counts fields a PERSON wrote, and a freshly resolved brain honestly reads 0/15. Making that countable was the whole problem, because `brand_memory` stores values and not authorship: the payload is a strict zod object with no room for per-field metadata, the table is read-only under RLS, and only wt-db may write a migration. Authorship turned out to be recoverable from what is already there — versions are append-only and each carries a `source`, so for every field you find the version at which its value last CHANGED and read that version's source. Two behaviours fall out of "last changed" rather than "last written" and both are the ones you want: a regenerate returning the SAME text for a confirmed field leaves the confirmation standing, because the model agreeing with you is not grounds to demote your answer; a regenerate returning DIFFERENT text reverts the field to a guess, because what is on screen is now the model's sentence and the ring must stop claiming otherwise. What this cost was a second write on Finish. A single combined save cannot carry provenance in either direction — stamped `resolved` it renders the user's own corrections back to them as machine guesses, stamped `manual` it marks all fifteen confirmed when they edited three — so onboarding now persists the model's output and the user's edits as two versions, and the diff between them is exactly the set of fields a person touched. Both halves go through the same save so both are pruned identically; an unpruned baseline against a pruned edit reports the two open-ended lists as changed and manufactures a confirmation nobody made. Three smaller things worth keeping. The Certainty System has four levels and forbids a fifth, and three concepts had to fit: confirmed is `.is-real` and guessed is `.is-proposed` (the rules' own sentence for the transition is "approving a proposed item turns the dash solid"), while DERIVED is not a certainty level at all — `alignment.signal_lock` is a conclusion drawn from the other fields, so it shows its evidence instead and sits outside the ring's denominator, because counting a question nobody can answer puts the ring permanently out of reach. The denominator must equal the editable set or the "most valuable unanswered question" eventually points at a field with no answer box, which is now a test asserting the two sets are identical rather than a fact someone checked once. And the negative guard that matters — editing must never re-resolve, or one sentence costs fifty credits AND demotes fourteen confirmed fields — cannot be proven by mocking, because the module that would charge is mocked wholesale in that test file; it is proven statically, by asserting `brand-field.ts`'s import lines never name `@sahoda/mesh`, `@sahoda/billing` or `resolveBrand`, with a fourth assertion that the import extraction found anything at all so an empty match cannot pass all three. Separately: the copy fix in the same commit removed "Red lines — the Loop will refuse these". Nothing refuses anything — red lines ride in the brand context the mesh prepends to every call, so they shape output and enforce nothing — and the place a user is most likely to trust a false guarantee is the card listing their legal and medical claims.
2026-08-12 · apps/web + packages/{shared,billing} (wt-limits) — **A gate that refuses the free plan correctly can be admitting every paid plan without bound, and the headline test cannot tell.** `checkEntitlement` shipped correct, tested and called from nowhere, so Free's `sites: 0` constrained nothing and 100 granted credits could buy a site the plan forbids. Mounting it is four lines; getting it RIGHT turns on one argument. `isAllowed` answers a weaker question when `currentUsage` is absent — `limit > 0`, "does the plan grant any of these at all" — which refuses Free by coincidence and returns true for Starter forever, however many sites exist. So the obvious fix closes the reported hole and leaves every paying tier unbounded, and a suite covering only Free signs off: mutation 2 (drop `currentUsage`) failed three tests and the free-plan test was one of the three that PASSED. That is the sibling-shape rule arriving through an OPTIONAL PARAMETER rather than a sibling call site, which is the third time this month an optional-with-a-plausible-default has been the defect. Ordering is the other half of the finding and it is not the same question at each site. For a paid action the commitment is the credit hold, so the gate goes above `withCredits` and "you were not charged" becomes true by construction rather than by hope. For a CHANNEL the commitment is the OAuth grant — and gating only the Zernio RETURN route, which is the only place that knows what came back, lands the refusal after the customer has already approved third-party access to their Instagram on Meta's own screen. Credits can be released; an external authorization cannot, so `start` has to refuse first, above `ensureZernioProfile` (which provisions a profile for a connect about to be declined). The return route still cannot refuse the trip: by then the account IS connected at Zernio, so it partitions on the upsert's own conflict key `(platform, accountId)` — a key already present is a refresh that consumes no allowance and must be written unconditionally, or the documented self-heal breaks and every repeat visit miscounts existing rows as new. Three smaller things. Enforcing a limit created two dead ends that did not exist while nothing enforced anything: there is no plan-upgrade surface in the product at all (only a credits top-up), and no way to delete a site, so a Starter customer's first draft is also their last — the honest response was to name the plan and link NOWHERE rather than invent an upgrade route or send them to `/wallet`, which sells the wrong thing. Limit copy must be DERIVED from `PLAN_CATALOG` by price, because the sentence anyone would write — "Sites are on Growth and above" — is false when Starter allows one. And putting the gate on a page render path made `loadBillingEnv`'s by-design throw into a 500 on two pages that render fine without billing configured, which the cloud sandbox (no `.env`) hits every time; `checkCountableLimit` now never throws and degrades to a verdict every caller already fails closed on. The atomicity the gate documents is still absent and still unbuildable here — three PostgREST inserts are not a transaction and only wt-db may add a constraint — and the pointer that was supposed to have filed it, "REQUESTS.md §8", named a file that does not exist.
2026-08-12 · packages/shared + packages/mesh + apps/jobs + apps/web (wt-gate) — **A rule that only reaches a prompt is a request, and the same model that was asked politely is the one being checked.** `taboo.red_lines` went into `brand-context.ts`'s cached prefix and nowhere else, so the Refine screen's "the Loop will refuse these" was a promise no code kept — the previous lane's fix was to delete the sentence, which is honest and leaves a regulated business unprotected. The gate is now a condition of publishing rather than a preflight, and the placement is the whole of it: `runPublishPost` is the one function all four entries reach through (the publish-now route, the cron sweep, the Trigger.dev task, and the bare `@sahoda/jobs/publish` export), so there is no rail that gets to an adapter around it. Four things generalise. FIRST, the dep is REQUIRED and not `gate?`, which is the third instance of that shape in this repo in a week (`viaZernio`, `lagHours`, `simulated`) and the only one caught before it shipped: adding it turned `tsc` red at `publish/deps.ts:31` and at the existing test harness immediately, and that compile error IS the proof that no publish path skips the check — an optional gate typechecks clean while doing nothing. SECOND, this file fails CLOSED inside a codebase that correctly fails open everywhere around it (`hostMedia` absent means `[]`, `readHashtags` on junk means undefined, "a shape we do not recognise is a reason to ignore the field, not to fail the publish"), so every unknown — unsure classifier, unreachable provider, unparseable answer, timeout, a rule id the model invented, a quote that is not literally in the post — resolves to HOLD, and the comment says it contradicts the neighbours on purpose because otherwise someone will tidy it back. THIRD, and this is the one that nearly went wrong: the obvious status for a held variant is `skipped`, and `skipped` is absent from `claimVariant`'s predicate — a held post could never be claimed again, so the writer would fix the wording, press Publish, and get a 409 reading "This post is already going out" about a post that never could, with no migration available to escape it. `failed` is the right landing because it IS claimable (rewrite-and-retry works) and `classifyCandidate` never re-dispatches it (`PENDING_STATES` is `pending|scheduled`), so the cron cannot loop on it either; the distinction between "a rule was broken" and "nobody could decide" lives in the CODE, and the verdict rides `last_error.gate` as STRUCTURE because `describePublishError` is an allowlist that never echoes a stored message — prose there arrives on screen as "something went wrong", which is the block that only says no. FOURTH, three honesty limits are recorded in code rather than smoothed over: onboarding discards the regime (`toResolveInput` folds it into prose), so every workspace today resolves to `consumer`/`default` and the mandated tier is the floor pack alone while the OWNER tier works fully; nothing anywhere records who APPROVED a post, so `audit_logs.meta.approver` is an explicit null and deliberately not the job-run id; and the packs are typed data authored in-repo from ordinary advertising norms, not doc 18 §4's markdown loader and not reviewed by counsel. Two smaller findings worth keeping. The word-boundary matcher cannot use `\b` — it is ASCII-defined, so on Devanagari every position is a boundary and any substring matches, and it also cannot match `#1` at all; `(?<![\p{L}\p{N}])` does both, and `cure` correctly ignores `secure`, `manicure` and `procedure`. And a mutation spec that runs one package's tests can report a comfortable 7/9 for the wrong reason: both survivors were verdict-layer mutants whose killing tests live in `packages/shared` and were simply never executed, which is the harness erring in the reassuring direction — the command now spans both packages and kills 9/9. Three findings came out of the review pass and all three were invisible to the suite that existed. The gate's SQL had never EXECUTED — every layer test injects `loadGateContext` as a function — and the failure direction is the silent one: a misplaced correlation in the lateral join returns no row, `loadGateContext` reports `{brandVersion: null, payload: null}`, and the composed gate treats that as a legitimate workspace that has written no red lines, so the floor pack still fires, nothing errors, and the OWNER tier quietly never applies while a passing test asserts that exact shape is valid; `store.pglite.test.ts` now runs it against real Postgres, including that a `draft` brain is ignored and that another tenant's brain is unreachable through a post id. A HOLD also turned out not to be one thing: held terminally, a five-minute provider outage would mark every post scheduled inside it permanently failed and need a person to press Publish on each — so `unavailable` and `timeout` are now recorded transient and rethrown (the claim comes back, the next tick retries, the grace window still expires it), while `unsure`, `unparseable` and `over-bounds` stay terminal because retrying re-asks a model that already could not tell, or fails identically forever. And `publishPostDeps()` had started constructing the mesh, which fails fast on missing provider keys: that throw would have surfaced as `PublishInfraError('deps')` → a 503 INFRA_UNAVAILABLE on every publish, indistinguishable from the database outage that stage classification was introduced to tell apart in the first place. Deferred into `classify()` — where `createMesh()`'s SYNCHRONOUS throw is caught, which a `.catch()` on the returned promise would have missed — the same missing key becomes a named `unavailable` hold instead. Last, a smaller honesty repair: the `worthRetrying: false` shipped on GATE_BLOCKED came with a comment claiming it stopped a retry button, and the field has no readers anywhere in apps/web — the affordance branches on `row.retryable`. A claim about a switch that is not a switch is worse than no comment.
