2026-08-13 · apps/web (wt-web) — **A UI rule that reads the roll-up instead of the record will contradict the widget two inches above it.** `autoPublishTruth` decided what to say under a scheduled post from `posts.status` plus a clock, and a clock cannot answer "did this go out": four production rows sat at `approved`/`scheduled` with a past date and published variants, and every one was told "this time has passed and nothing was published — copy it across to post it", printed directly beneath its own channel chips reading "published". An instruction to publish a post again, under the evidence that it was already out. The record is `post_variants.publish_status`; the post-level status rolls up late or not at all, and `65dc1a34` proved it by sitting at `scheduled` with both of its variants at `published`. What makes this worth writing down is the near-miss in the fix. The obvious repair — count `published` variants, and if any, stop saying nothing published — changes what NOTHING renders, because all eight published variants on those four posts ran on the fixture rail: `publish_status = 'published'`, `permalink = fixture://…`, no platform ever involved. A repair that folds simulated into live would have shipped a variant-reading code path exercised by zero production rows and proved by mutation against injected inputs only, which is the exact dead-code shape this file's own header already records about the PREVIOUS version of the same gate (it read `status === 'scheduled'`, a status apps/web has never written, and rendered for nobody while every unit test passed). The discriminating question is not "does the fix pass tests" but "does the fix change what a named production row renders", and it has to be asked before the design is settled, because both answers pass a green suite. So `simulated` is counted apart from `live` and gets its own sentence naming the simulation, which is the only reading that is true of the platforms AND true of the chips. Two smaller rules fell out. An empty variant list is not evidence of no publish — `listVariantStates` returns an empty map on any read failure and `.get()` cannot distinguish that from a post with no variants, so it yields the weaker claim rather than `overdue`, or a variant-read outage revives the whole defect on every card at once. And the rows parameter is required in position on both the function and the component: three surfaces already held the data and the week grid was already forwarding it to its overflow rows and not to its own day cells, so the optional shape would have kept exactly one of them silently wrong. Ten mutants, ten killed; three of them are the wiring, which no test of the rule alone would ever notice.
2026-08-13 · packages/shared + apps/web (wt-web, merging wt-gate) — **A gate can be fully built, fully tested, and enforce nothing, because the one input that selects its rules was never written down.** The refusal gate resolves its mandated rule set from `regime × locale`. Onboarding collected exactly that, classified it deterministically with a per-field basis, showed the customer the read-back — and then folded it into a prose sentence for the resolve prompt (`REGIME_NOUN` inside a string) and kept no structure. So `intakeFrom()` found no intake on any workspace that has ever existed, every one resolved to `consumer`/`default`, and the mandated tier was the floor pack alone: a clinic that picked "Health & care" was judged by the general advertising floor while the Refine screen promised its red lines were enforced. The fix needs no migration — `resolve_brand_memory` validates six sections and ignores the rest, the same seam `field_meta` rides — but it needs two things that are easy to miss. First, the BASIS has to travel with the value, because the refusal copy branches on it: `declared` licenses "this comes with the trade you told us you are in" and anything weaker must say "this applies to every business", so storing a regime without recording how strongly it was held lets the product attribute a rule to a regulator nobody consulted. The classifier's own vocabulary maps one-to-one (`chosen`→declared, `matched`→derived, `assumed`→not stored at all), and refusing to store `assumed` is what makes the resume path correct for free: a user returning to a saved brain has no sentence and no overrides, every verdict is `assumed`, the mapper returns null, and their original declaration survives. Second, and this is the half that rots quietly, `BrandMemoryPayloadSchema` has no `intake` key and `saveBrandMemory` parses through it — so without an explicit carry-forward from the row being replaced, the first single-field edit on /brain strips the regime and returns that clinic to the floor pack, with nothing on any screen to say it happened. A persisted field that the write path does not deliberately preserve is a field with a half-life. Both halves are pinned by mutation (7/7), and one mutant exists only to catch the failure the other six cannot: Finish never calling the mapper at all, which would leave a correct, tested function that nobody invokes — the same shape `checkEntitlement` had for three weeks before wt-limits mounted it.
2026-08-13 · ops (wt-web) — **Two production facts that look like a contradiction are both true when one of them is a lease.** The cron config reported `publishEnabled: true` while `publish_claimed_at` was NULL on all 36 `post_variants`, which reads as "the switch is on and nothing has ever claimed a row". Neither half is wrong. `publish_claimed_at` is meaningful ONLY in the pair `(publish_status = 'publishing', claimed_at)` — its own migration comment says "cleared on every terminal outcome" — so NULL everywhere is what a queue with nothing in flight looks like, not evidence that nothing ever ran. `publishing` is 0, which is the corroborating read. The switch is separately confirmed to be doing work: `post_publish_logs` carries 4 rows stamped `cron:<postId>` on 2026-08-10, three `succeeded`/`live` on instagram and one `failed`/`live` on linkedin, against 11 stamped `web:` from manual publish-now. So scheduled auto-publish HAS published to real accounts from the cron path. The general rule: before reporting a state column as evidence of history, read its own comment for whether it is a lease, a latch or a log — a lease is cleared by success, so its emptiness is the shape of things having gone right. The history lives in the append-only table, and the `job_run_id` prefix is what separates the cron from the browser.

2026-08-13 · apps/web (wt-hide) — **A scope decision recorded as a defect is a different lie from a defect recorded as a pass, and the fix is not to edit the number.** `/sites` was in the rail while its deploy half is unowned — generation and preview work, `sites.status` never leaves `'draft'`, and no Cloudflare client exists — so the nav promised an address a customer could never have, and the Alpha panel counted A12 among "6 of 14 failing" as though someone had tried and failed. The obvious repair was to drop `'A12'` from `ALPHA_GATE.failingCodes`, and it was wrong for a reason the file states in its own header: every field there is a TRANSCRIPTION of a dated human judgement, not a computation. `recordedOn: '2026-07-25'` and `source: 'wt-web audit'` stay put, so a six-to-five edit would make the 25 Jul audit claim it found five failures. What it found was six. The shape that survives both truths is a second dated record — `outOfScope: [{ code, decidedOn, reason }]`, decided 13 Aug by a different person on a different day — with the subtraction done in `AlphaChip` where a reader can watch it happen. Two things that came with it and are the actual load-bearing parts: the drop is stated on the COLLAPSED chip face (`5 of 14 failing · 1 out of scope`), because a count that falls with the reason hidden behind a click is the same silence the existing `unmatched` guard exists to prevent; and the descope list got its own mismatch guard, since a typo in a descope code is now the one way a real failure can leave the count unnoticed. The hide itself was one line — `typedRoutes` types `href` as `Route`, so removing a nav entry can never dangle, while the reverse (a route with no nav entry) is invisible to the compiler, which is exactly what makes "hidden but reachable by URL" a safe resting state for code that is waiting rather than dead. Two landmines left for whoever lands A14: the seeded `site.first_tour` still promises "build + deploy it" and now triggers on first visit to a nav-hidden route, and `PENDING_ANCHORS['connections.connect_x']` still gives "connecting is blocked, no INSERT policy" as its reason when all four platforms have since gone live through Zernio — a stale reason on a passing test is how the last five tours drifted.

2026-08-14 · scripts + packages/shared + apps/web (wt-ingest) — **A transport failure hides every content failure behind it, and fixing the transport is when you find out how many there were.** The board sync had posted to `app.sahodalabs.com` for a week. That host is not this project and never has been: Vercel's edge answers `DEPLOYMENT_NOT_FOUND`, which is the platform saying the domain is registered to Vercel with NO deployment aliased behind it — not a rival app returning 404, nothing at all. Correcting one constant was the whole of the reported bug, and it was the smallest part of the work, because the very first sync that reached a live server failed again on a defect that had been sitting there for days: `detail` capped at `max(2000)` in `packages/shared` while twelve cards were over it and the longest was 4763. The schema is `.strict()` over the whole payload, so one long card rejects all 83 — board, changelog and QA stop together and no individual file looks guilty. A third violation (`summary_plain` at 621/600) was invisible even then, because the 400 echoes issue PATHS and the error line truncates them; finding them from responses means one violation per round trip. The generalisable move was to stop asking the server: parse the exact payload `buildPayload()` constructs against the real schema LOCALLY and print every issue at once. That is now `state-sendable.test.ts`, and it earned its place the same hour by catching a FOURTH defect nobody had reasoned to — `qa` is capped at `.max(200)` in the schema while `ops-hook-bash.mjs` trims the queue with `.slice(-200)`, identical ceilings with zero headroom, so the automatic path parks at the limit forever and a single manual `/qa-log` (the documented way to record manual testing) tips it to 201 and refuses the entire payload. Filed as SL-084. Three things worth carrying beyond this repo. FIRST, the probe that settles a 401 is the one that cannot write: real token plus a deliberately unparseable body returns `invalid_json` from one line PAST the constant-time token check and short of any write, which proves reachability AND credential validity in a single request that is structurally incapable of mutating anything — where a bare 401 cannot distinguish our token gate from a deployment-protection wall, and both are 401. SECOND, a queue that evicts is not a queue: the card promised 'nothing has been lost — the updates replay once this is fixed', which is TRUE of the board and changelog (uncapped) and FALSE of QA, where 140 runs committed at abaf161 were already gone from the working tree, shredded by the ring buffer while the endpoint was dead. A ring buffer pointed at a dead endpoint is a shredder, and the reassuring sentence is what stopped anyone looking. THIRD, `turbo typecheck lint test` reported 27/27 in 83ms with `Cached: 27` on this diff — the edit was under `scripts/`, which no package hashes, so a green count was produced by a tree nothing had checked; the honest coverage was `test:ops`, `format:check` and a mutation that restores the dead host and must fail. Two operational notes. The replay was driven through `ops_ingest` — the same RPC the route calls — which lands the real rows but has no zod in front of it, and that is exactly why the 201-run payload sailed through when the deployed route would have refused it: a shortcut around validation buys the outcome and forfeits the check. And `extensions.worktreeConfig=true` means a new worktree is BORN with a `config.worktree` shadowing the repo-level author; plain `git config user.name` writes the repo file and reads back the old value, silently, which is the actual mechanism behind the wt-limits IDIVASM commit — only `git config --worktree` takes.

2026-08-14 · scripts + packages/shared + apps/web (wt-qafix) — **A cap on a queue is a retention policy; a cap on a payload is a wire limit; when one number does both jobs, fixing either one breaks the other.** `recordQaRuns` ended with `.slice(-200)`, which reads as a ring buffer over recent activity and was in fact a ring buffer over an OUTBOX: rows leave `qa.pending.json` only on an ack, so everything in it is unsent by definition. Pointed at a dead host for a week the drain never ran, the queue parked at its ceiling, and every new run deleted the oldest unsent one — 140 destroyed by the code whose job was to keep them. THE TRAP IS THE OBVIOUS FIX. Deleting the slice does not work, and the reason is two files away: `OpsIngestPayloadSchema` caps `qa` at 200 and the route is `.strict()`, so 201 rows is a 400 on the WHOLE payload — board and roadmap with it — and `ingestVerdict` correctly classifies a 400 as PERMANENT. An uncapped writer therefore trades a queue that shreds for one that wedges shut for ever, which is strictly worse because it is silent in both directions. So the number was split in two: WIRE_BATCH_MAX (200, the server's, honoured by sending the oldest batch) and QUEUE_CEILING (1000, ours, bounded by BYTES rather than ambition — these files are committed and rewritten by the formatter after every tool call, and a QA row carries up to 4000 characters of test tail). SL-084's own card proposed 'apply the trim in the writer'; the implementation refuses instead of trimming, because the same card rejects trim-at-send on the grounds that it 'drops a QA run that was never delivered' — which condemns a trim at the writer for exactly the same reason. At the ceiling the NEW row is refused and the refusal shouts: the queued rows are irreplaceable and the refused one is still in the scrollback, so evicting the old for the new is precisely the trade that lost the 140. A SECOND SHREDDER WAS HIDING BEHIND THE FIRST: the ack path called `clearPending`, which wiped the file. That is safe only while every POST carries the whole queue, so the moment batching landed it would have deleted every unsent row past 200 on the first SUCCESSFUL sync — and it had always lost any run recorded during the 8-second request window. `dropSent` removes the acknowledged PREFIX by position, deliberately not by matching `client_id`: a missing or duplicated id would match nothing, remove zero rows, and wedge the queue via identity instead of via size. Three findings about proving it. The mutation spec was 9/10 first run and the survivor was the false-success guard — the assertion was `not.toContain('ops: QA unit pass')` and the mutant prints `ops: QA ` with an empty suite list, which is not that string and is exactly the impression the guard exists to prevent; the ASSERTION was tightened rather than the mutant dropped, which is the only honest direction. Loudness is the property a suite never asserts, so it needed the real script: `OPS_REPO_ROOT` was added purely so a test can spawn the hook against a temp directory, and `fetch` is replaced inside the child by `node --import` rather than by a seam in the script — a socket test self-skips or hangs the first time it meets a restricted sandbox, and a test that never runs counted as one that passed is already a card in this repo. AND THE GATE DOES NOT RUN ANY OF IT. Measured, not derived: `turbo run typecheck lint test --dry=json` hashes 1150 of 1316 tracked files, and the 166 outside every task's inputs include ALL of `scripts/`, `ops/`, `docs/`, `tsconfig.base.json`, `vitest.config.ts` and — worst — `pricing.config.json`, which `packages/shared/src/ledger/pricing.ts` reads and two suites assert against, so a credit-price edit ships with FULL TURBO green. Appending a newline to each of those changes 0 of 27 task hashes; the same append to a package source changes 19. `turbo.json` is a special case worth stating precisely: whitespace changes 0, a real task-definition change changes 9. Separately from the hash gap, `turbo run test` never executes the root vitest project at all — `vitest.config.ts` says so in its own header — so the 162 tests here are real only because they were run by hand and by the mutation harness. Last, a mechanism that has quietly never fired for this lane's style of commit: `closingTaskCodes` reads the conventional-commit SCOPE only and accepts only `feat`/`fix`, so `fix(ops): SL-084 …` closes nothing while `fix(SL-084): …` closes the card — and the `-m "…"` form never matches at all, because the anchored subject regex cannot see past `git commit -m "`. 38 of 43 done cards carry a sha; the five that do not are the ones whose closing commit put the code in the subject text.

2026-08-14 · turbo.json + packages/shared (wt-web) — **A guard that lives outside every hash input is not a guard, and the only honest way to show it is to break the thing it guards and watch the gate stay green.** wt-qafix measured the gap and named the worst instance; this closes it. `packages/shared/src/ledger/pricing.ts` reaches the config with `import rawPricing from '../../../../pricing.config.json'` — four levels up, out of the package entirely. Turbo builds a task hash from the files INSIDE that package plus its declared dependencies, so a relative import that escapes the package boundary is opaque to the hasher even though TypeScript resolves it without complaint; the file belonged to no package, and root `turbo.json` declared no `globalDependencies` key at all. Measured rather than argued: with the config unfixed, moving `post_variants` from 3 to 7 changed 0 of 16 task hashes and `turbo test` answered 16/16 cached, FULL TURBO in 24ms — a wrong credit price, fully green, with both asserting suites replayed from cache. `globalDependencies: ["pricing.config.json"]` mixes the file into the GLOBAL hash, which every task hash inherits regardless of whether that task declares `inputs`; the identical mutation now moves 16 of 16. THE TEMPTING NARROWER FIX IS A SECOND GAP. Turbo 2.x accepts `"$TURBO_ROOT$/pricing.config.json"` inside a task's `inputs`, which looks surgical and is a trap: adding an `inputs` array switches that task from 'hash every file in the package' to 'hash only these globs', so an edit meant to make the suite depend on MORE would have made it depend on LESS and opened a fresh hole behind the one just closed. Global invalidation is also the correct blast radius on the merits — a credit price reaches costs-shown-before-spend, wallet copy and entitlements, so busting everything is proportionate for a CLAUDE.md non-negotiable. Two things about proving it. The fix EDITS `turbo.json`, which busts the global hash by itself, so the first run after it is a guaranteed full miss that demonstrates nothing; the load-bearing step is the SECOND run on an unchanged tree returning FULL TURBO, because only a confirmed-warm cache makes the subsequent miss attributable to the price. And re-running is the weaker half of the claim: a suite that executes and still passes is not a guard, so each suite was failed on the price it actually pins — `ledger/pricing.test.ts` on `post_variants` (expected 7 to be 3, and 14 to be 6 through the units multiplier), `mesh/tasks.test.ts` on `image_standard` (expected 9 to be 6). They guard DIFFERENT keys, so one mutation only ever reddens one of them, and a single experiment reported as 'both suites re-run' would have been true and misleading. Still open and deliberately untouched here: `scripts/`, `ops/`, `docs/`, `tsconfig.base.json` and `vitest.config.ts` remain outside every task's inputs, and `turbo run test` still never executes the root vitest project — that is the runner question, not the hash question, and it wants its own card.

2026-08-14 · packages/mesh + packages/shared (wt-web) — **A column applied to production is not a measurement; nothing was writing it, and the seam that "already knew" was an observer nobody had subscribed to.** `ai_provider_logs.repaired` shipped on 2026-08-12 to expose the 83% repair rate on `brand_extract`, and read `false` on all 189 rows because no writer ever set it. The tempting wiring was `MeshRunnerDeps.onRepair`, added during the same diagnosis and genuinely holding the fact — and it is the wrong seam, for a reason that is invisible from inside the engine: `onRepair` is a CALLER-INJECTED observer, and every production call site (`apps/jobs/src/ai/mesh.ts:11`, and the `meshSingleton ??= createMesh()` in each of `apps/web/src/app/actions/{posts-ai,posts-image,plan-week,site-generate,onboarding-resolve}.ts`) calls `createMesh()` bare. Only a live diagnosis test ever passed the callback, so routing telemetry through it would have written the column in the one context that did not need it and nowhere that did. The write belongs on the row the engine already builds: `toLogRow` (`packages/mesh/src/engine.ts:166`), driven by `firstAttemptError !== null`. THE ENFORCEMENT MATTERED MORE THAN THE WRITE. `repaired` was added to `AiProviderLogSchema` as `z.boolean()` — required, not `.optional()`, not nullable — and because `ProviderLogRow = Omit<AiProviderLog,'id'|'created_at'>`, the compiler then refused all nine `writeLog` sites until each stated an answer, including the four on image and outage paths that will always be `false`. An optional field defaulting to `false` would have compiled in silence and recreated the exact blindness the column exists to end; this repo has already shipped two defects from `lagHours?` and `simulated?` doing precisely that. The type change caught `telemetry.test.ts:19` immediately, and then did NOT catch the thing beside it: `ALLOWED_COLUMNS`, a runtime allow-list asserting telemetry never ships a payload column, which would have rejected `repaired` at test time with no compile error anywhere. A new column on a privacy-guarded table has to be admitted twice, and only one of the two guards is a type. VERIFYING IT BY OUTCOME RAN INTO A REAL WALL, worth stating plainly rather than smoothing over: the fix that motivated the column also removed the ability to demonstrate it. Seven unforced live extractions today on the same fbs.edu.in corpus that repaired 5-in-6 on 2026-08-12 repaired 0 times, so a naturally-repaired row is no longer obtainable on demand — a model that has stopped making the mistake cannot be asked to make it. The `true` case was therefore produced by perturbing the FIRST provider response in transit while the request, the model, the repair retry, the second charge and the telemetry write all stayed real, and it is labelled as a forced branch everywhere it appears, because evidence that the write path carries `true` is not evidence about how often extraction repairs. AND THE 83% → 0% IS NOT A ONE-VARIABLE RESULT. Commit `5fa478d` raised `MAX_TOKENS` 2048 → 4096, shipped a stripper cutting 22% of input tokens, AND slimmed the wire contract so the model stopped writing `confirmed` and cited a page index instead of repeating a long `source_url` per field — three changes to the same ratio in one commit. The row data reconciles exactly and shows it: pre-fix single-pass input 13,119 tokens, post-fix 10,193 (−22.3%, the stripper), while every repaired row reads ≈2× its clean twin (26,777 ≈ 2×13,119; today's forced row 20,449 ≈ 2×10,193 + 63 for the repair prompt). That doubling is what a repair costs, and it was only ever inferable from token arithmetic before there was a column to group by. THE VERIFICATION PERMANENTLY POISONS THE TABLE IT VERIFIED, and there is no undo: `ai_provider_logs` carries `create trigger block_mutations before update or delete`, so the one forced row cannot be deleted or corrected and every future repair-rate query counts it. As written that is 1/53 = 1.9% for `brand_extract` — a fabricated rate produced by the session that took care not to fabricate one. EVERY synthetic row this table holds has a `trace_id` beginning `repair` (`repair-diag-*` from the 2026-08-12 diagnosis, `repaired-verify-*` and `repaired-forced-*` from this one), so the honest query is `where trace_id is null or trace_id not like 'repair%'`, and that predicate belongs here rather than in a commit message nobody queries from. The general rule: a live test that writes to an append-only production table is choosing to alter that table's statistics for ever, and it owes the next reader a discriminator at the time it writes, not an apology afterwards. THE DENOMINATOR IS THE PART TO BE HONEST ABOUT. 76 of 197 rows predate any repair signal whatsoever and their rate is not merely 0 but unknowable — the migration comment says exactly this, that `false` on an old row means "not measured", never "ran clean" — and reporting a whole-table rate would have averaged real zeroes with absent data. Owed and deliberately not done here: `20260812000000_ai_provider_logs_repaired.sql` exists only on `wt-db3` and its commit `692f0bd` bundles an unrelated 293-line `resolve_brand_memory_v2` migration, so cherry-picking it would drag that lane's work across; the column is live in production and the repo's migration history does not describe the deployed schema until wt-db3 merges.

2026-08-19 · packages/db + apps/web + apps/jobs (wt-ui-port) — **A runtime feature detector must read something it can observe; when the only signal available was an error code nobody here had ever seen, the right move was to delete the branch, not to guess the string.** Six migrations were drafted for the founder to apply by hand, and the code for two of them had to work correctly BEFORE and AFTER the apply. The obvious detector — call the new RPC, catch the failure, fall back to the old write — needs a match on "function does not exist", which Postgres spells `42883` and the PostgREST layer above it spells something else that no test, fixture or recording in this repo contains. Guessing wrong there is not a missed feature: it silently turns every genuine save failure into a last-write-wins upsert that reports success, which is precisely the defect the change existed to remove, reintroduced by its own safety net and invisible because the save looks fine. So the fallback was written and then deleted, and a failed call is now a failed save; the state it guarded (column present, function absent) cannot really arise because a migration file applies as a unit. THE DETECTOR THAT REPLACED IT READS PRESENCE, NOT ERRORS. `PostVariantSchema` is frozen and strips unknown columns, so `version` never reaches a screen — but the raw rows the post page ALREADY fetches either carry the key or they do not, and `'version' in row` cannot be wrong about a database it is looking straight at, costs no extra query, and needs no code to match. Its one blind spot is a post with NO variants: no rows carry no columns, and answering "unsupported" there would put the FIRST save of a brand-new post back on last-write-wins — the save two tabs are most likely to make at the same moment. That case asks the table directly and reads WHETHER it was refused, never why. The same two-question shape settled the analytics half: if the history read is refused, read a table that certainly exists; if THAT answers, the database is reachable and the refusal was about the missing table. Confirmed by execution, not inference — a real browser against real PostgREST rendered "does not keep a history yet" and did not render "could not read the history", which is the discriminator working end to end. Both detectors are memoised for five minutes, because before the migration they are refused on EVERY page load and the answer changes at most once ever; only the negative verdict is cached, never data and never a failure, so the staleness runs in the safe direction and clears without a deploy. DOC 23's SQL WOULD HAVE BROKEN THE FIRST SAVE OF EVERY CHANNEL. Its compare-and-set only UPDATEs, while today's `saveVariant` is an upsert — so with the plan's SQL exactly as printed, creating a channel's copy returns no row and is reported as a conflict with a writer who does not exist. The migration adds a create arm (`p_expected_version is null` → `insert … on conflict do nothing`), which is still a compare-and-set — creating is a save against "no row yet" — and every divergence from the plan is named in the file, because a founder diffing the two must not find an undocumented branch. `returns setof` replaced `returns public.post_variants` for a related reason: the plan says the function must return NOTHING on a clash, and a NULL composite crossing the API can arrive looking like a row whose every field is blank, which reads as a successful save of empty text. THREE THINGS ABOUT PROVING ANY OF IT. First, the SQL is EXECUTED, and against the real migration files rather than another hand-written DDL — `packages/db/tests/helpers/pglite-schema.ts` boots the actual `.sql` off disk, which caught the class of error that is invisible in review and instantly fatal in a terminal: removing A5's `unique (id, workspace_id)` turns all five batch checks red, exactly as it would turn the founder's apply red. Second, an equivalent mutant is not a failed detector and must not be dressed as one: dropping the explicit conflict target from `on conflict (…) do nothing` changed nothing, because the bare form catches the same violation WHILE the constraint exists — the two differ only if the daily rule is ever dropped, so the test that kills it drops the constraint and asserts the write refuses loudly rather than silently writing a second row and doubling every total. Third, a test harness is code and gets the same defects: `over.platform_post_id ?? DEFAULT` silently replaced an explicit `null`, so the case asserting "a variant with no platform id is skipped" inserted an id and passed for the wrong reason — the same optional-param silent-default class this repo has now shipped three times, this time in the thing meant to catch it. AND THE GATE CAUGHT WHAT LOCAL RUNS COULD NOT, TWICE. Two typecheck errors in test files that vitest ran happily (a mock's return type narrowed to one arm of a union; a non-generic stub failing a generic interface) — `npx vitest` is not `tsc --noEmit` and running one is not running the other. Then a genuine resource finding: the new PGlite suites booted about fifty WebAssembly Postgres instances between them, ~3s of CPU each, and under `turbo run test` — where every package's suite runs at once — that starved apps/web into 5s-default timeouts in files that had nothing to do with this work. Reusing one instance per block with `truncate` between tests, and applying each migration inside a transaction and rolling it back rather than rebooting for each, took it to about six (packages/db 71.8s → 30.8s, the jobs store suite 46.9s → 4.7s) — with an assertion that the rollback really rolled back, since without it every file after the first would have been applied on top of its siblings and the test would assert the opposite of its own name. One failure survived that and was NOT mine: parking all four new suites and re-running reproduced it, so `not-connected-vs-unreadable.test.ts` pays its own `vi.resetModules()` + dynamic-import cost against a 5s budget, and the budget was raised with the measurement written next to it. THE LAST FINDING IS THE ONE THAT CHANGED A DOCUMENT RATHER THAN CODE. `docs/24_Migration_Batch.md` told the founder A2 was the urgent item because every day without it is history that can never be recovered — true of the TABLE, and misleading about the outcome, because nothing runs the job that fills it: apps/jobs has never been deployed to Trigger.dev, and the metric pass cannot join the five-minute sweep that actually runs, since one request per published channel against a table keyed by DAY would spend the rate limit to write nothing 287 times out of 288. Applying A2 starts nothing; wiring a daily tick does. The core is exported from the SDK-free entry point so that wiring is small, and the doc now says which step is the urgent one. Related, and the reason the run report can be trusted at all: `written: 0` is the healthy answer for a second run in one day AND exactly what a frozen `lastUpdated` looks like every night for ever — and the day bucket is derived from that stamp, which `analytics-state.ts` warns is safe to print and unsafe to reason about per post. The report now carries the newest stamp it saw, so a stall reads differently from a repeat.

2026-08-19 · apps/web + apps/jobs + packages/publishing (wt-ui-port) — **A proof in an in-memory Postgres is a proof about SQL, and the six migrations going live turned four of them back into open questions.** The compare-and-set had been executed in PGlite and shipped behind a runtime detector, and NONE of that establishes that the running app reaches the function: the version column is stripped by a frozen row schema before any screen sees it, the detection was written months before the column existed, and every failure mode is silent — a legacy upsert refuses nothing and moves nothing, so "still on the old path" looks exactly like "working". The discriminator that settles it needs no instrumentation and is the whole trick: **the old write cannot move `version`.** Two browser CONTEXTS (not tabs — one storage state shares a cookie jar and masks a per-session bug), a service-role read-back because no screen renders that counter, and the counter reaching 2 after a second save is the measurement. Forcing `expectedVersionFor` to return undefined turns all three red with version stuck at 1 and no notice ever drawn, which is the original defect reproduced against production. The FIRST save is the case worth keeping: docs/23's SQL as printed only UPDATEs while the real write is an upsert, so it would have reported every channel's first save as a clash with a writer who does not exist. And cross-tenant was proven with the SERVICE ROLE deliberately — RLS bypassed — so what is under test is the function's own `where workspace_id =` rather than the policies above it; `pg_get_functiondef` read back and matched the file on all six load-bearing properties, which is the only way to know the deployed function is the one that was reviewed. THE RUNNER WAS THE REAL WORK, NOT THE JOB. doc 24 had already been corrected to say applying A2 starts nothing, and this run found the second half: `trigger.dev whoami` answers "You must login first", because `TRIGGER_SECRET_KEY` is a `tr_dev_` SDK runtime key and the deploy CLI wants a personal access token — a founder action. Separately `trigger.config.ts` read `TRIGGER_PROJECT_REF`, set nowhere; the env and turbo.json both name `TRIGGER_PROJECT_ID`, so the first deploy would have targeted the empty string while the comment beside it promised deploys would "fail loudly". Both were invisible for the same reason: nothing has ever been deployed, so the config had never been executed. The pass now rides the runner that demonstrably exists — a Vercel cron, daily, its own route because a day-keyed table on the five-minute tick spends one Zernio request per published channel 288 times to write nothing on 287 of them. Run against production three times: 18 rows, then 0, then 0. Idempotent on the REAL table, and the rows read back are real channels and real values with ZERO all-zero series — the single failing target wrote nothing rather than a row of noughts, which is the one rule that would be permanent if broken, because a stored zero outlives every screen that refuses to draw one. `unreadable` was one number doing two jobs and production showed it immediately: split, it answers `unreadable: 1, unresolved: 0`, so the CALL fails and the platform is not disowning the post — opposite responses, and a retry only ever fixes one. FOUR GUARDS CAUGHT ME, AND THE THIRD ONE HAD NEVER WORKED. (1) `no-impossible-remedy.spec.ts` caught /analytics telling a workspace-less account "could not read the history — reload to try again"; nothing had failed and reloading cannot make a workspace, which is the same "could not look" / "looked and found nothing" collapse `lib/inbox/emptiness.ts` exists for, arriving through a new read that had not learned the rule. (2) `wiring.test.ts` caught the second cron entry — and repairing it revealed that its own parse split the public-route list on commas and kept whatever started with `/api`, so an entry preceded by an explanatory comment landed in the same segment as that comment and was filtered out. MEASURED by printing the parse: `/api/webhooks/cashfree`, a PUBLIC endpoint taking payment webhooks, had never been checked by the guard whose own comment claimed a new public /api route "still fails here first". It did not. Stripping comments before the split is what makes that sentence true, and a route hidden behind a comment now fails it. (3) `sweeps.test.ts` fired the moment `runPublishPost` imported `@sahoda/publishing`, because the SQL-only serverless bundle could then reach it. Every edge was `import type` and erased at runtime, so teaching the walker to ignore type imports would have been the quick fix and the wrong one — a type-only import is one refactor from a value import. `PublishMode`, a two-value union, moved to a leaf and the edge is gone. (4) prettier caught a scratch file of mine sitting in packages/db, which is the format:check-outside-turbo blind spot working in the other direction for once. AND MY OWN TEST HARNESS SHIPPED THE `??` DEFECT AGAIN, in the run after the one that recorded it: `data?.format ?? 'STILL-SET'` fires when format IS null, so the sentinel replaced the very value being asserted and the check could never pass. Presence of the ROW is what a sentinel is for; the field is what is being measured. Third instance of this class in this repo, twice now inside the thing meant to catch it. THE FORMAT COLUMN IS SMALLER THAN IT LOOKS, and saying so is the deliverable. `PlatformSpec` has no format dimension and `mediaTypes` is image-only on all four channels, so video cannot be ENFORCED and therefore is not offered — every rule is derived from the spec instead of listed beside it, and a test proves the derivation by giving a copy of the spec a `video/mp4` and watching the refusal stop. What a format actually buys is a promise publishing keeps: the Constraint Engine checks media against the CHANNEL and finds an image perfectly legal on X, so only the declared intent knows the writer did not mean to send one — and a photo post with no photo publishes today as bare text and reports success. Both refusals sit one layer ABOVE the adapter, forced rather than chosen: `PublishRequest` and `FormattedContent` are frozen with no field that could carry a format, so the natural home beside MEDIA_REQUIRED needs a contract change. The picker offers the INTERSECTION across selected channels, never the union, because the choice is written to all of them; and NOTHING is preselected, which is load-bearing — publishing now refuses a contradiction, so a default would write an intent nobody expressed onto every post and the first casualty would be an ordinary text post that has published fine for months.

2026-08-19 · apps/web + apps/jobs + packages (wt-ui-port, second run) — **The branch did not build, and the gate could not see it: `turbo build` is in none of `pnpm gate`'s four halves, and typecheck cannot find this class because the types resolve perfectly.** `create-flow.tsx` is `'use client'` and had been given a VALUE import of `formatsFor` from the `@sahoda/publishing` barrel; the barrel re-exports the X OAuth helper, which imports `node:crypto`, and webpack answered `UnhandledSchemeError` with a four-line trace naming the exact chain. The inbox has imported that same barrel for months and is fine — because its import is `import type` and erased before bundling. That distinction is the entire defect and the entire fix: a leaf entry point (`@sahoda/publishing/format`, whose only import is a type) plus a guard that fails a client component value-importing a Node-reaching barrel while leaving the erased forms legal. Banning both would be a rule the codebase breaks on its first read, and a rule that gets broken teaches people to ignore the rest. The guard's OWN first regex was wrong and announced itself: a lazy `[\s\S]*?` clause spanned from a file's first import to the matching specifier, swallowing every import between, so the "is this type-only" test read the whole file and always answered no — it went red on a file known-correct for months, which is a guard's first job. The clause may not contain a quote now. **A GUARD NEVER SHOWN TO FAIL IS NOT A GUARD, AND FIVE OF THEM COULD NOT SEE WHAT THEY ASSERTED.** Run 26 found `wiring.test.ts` skipping any entry preceded by a comment, leaving the public Cashfree payment webhook unchecked. Asking the same question of every other guard — by RUNNING each parser against every import shape a file can contain, rather than reading the code — found the identical hole in five: `billing/server-webhook.guard`, `cashfree/import-graph.guard`, `jobs/backfill/import-graph.guard` and `web/server-event-handler.guard` were all blind to a side-effect `import './x'`, a dynamic `import('./x')` and a `require('./x')`; `jobs/sweeps.test` saw everything but CJS. Every one of those exists to prove something UNREACHABLE — the fixture provider's well-known HMAC secret from a public webhook, the publish path from a backfill that walks every published variant — so a dynamic import reached it and the guard stayed green. All five widened, each shown to fail on the shape it used to miss. `from` keeps its REQUIRED whitespace in the apps/web guards, because `from\s*\(` reads every one of the hundreds of `supabase.from('posts')` calls as a module specifier; the paren shapes are matched under `import`/`require` instead, and one contrived false POSITIVE remains (the word `import` inside a quoted string) which fails closed. Separately, `service-rpc.test.ts` guards doc 13 §16's promise that the ingest token cannot reach credits by listing every `.rpc('name')` — matching a SINGLE-QUOTED literal only, so `.rpc("x")`, a template literal and `.rpc(variable)` were not disallowed but UNSEEN, which reads identically to absent. The fix is not a wider regex, because no regex reads `.rpc(name)`: anything unreadable is now REFUSED. **THE SENTINEL CLASS, SWEPT PROPERLY.** 430 test files, seven shapes, twenty candidates, each read rather than counted: fourteen in the two CREDIT-SPENDING actions asserted `ok: false` and left `insufficient` free, so an action answering `insufficient: true` skipped the entire branch and the test passed having checked neither that the customer was told they were not charged nor that provider text stayed out of the message — the two things those tests exist for. One was mine, a sort assertion inside `if (kind === 'ready')` with no assertion on `kind` at all. Five were legitimately guarded (if/else pairs, or a preceding `toMatchObject` naming both discriminants) and are recorded as accepted rather than silently dropped. Proven three ways, including an isolated demonstration that the OLD assertion passes on the mutated value with the branch never running — because a mutation that reddens several tests at once does not prove which assertion did the work. **AND THE HIGHEST-PRIORITY ARCHITECTURAL ITEM TURNED OUT TO BE ALREADY BUILT.** The standing instruction said the pre-flight workspace assertion before a Zernio publish is NOT BUILT and that publishing must stay shut until it is. `public.assert_account_for_scheduled_post` has been applied since 2026-08-01, replaced twice since, and re-derives the workspace FROM THE POST — there is no workspace argument to fill in — returning an account id only when an ACTIVE connection, same workspace, same channel as the variant, under that workspace's Zernio profile, owns it. Six hostile shapes against production all refused, including one workspace's real post with another workspace's real active Instagram account. **Read the applied artifact before building the thing it describes**: the file NAMED after that function is the stale definition, and a test built on it produced a function refusing `approved` posts and every channel but Instagram. What was actually broken was the AUDIT: the assertion refuses by raising, and every throw out of `resolveConnection` was filed as `CONNECTION_UNAVAILABLE`, so a deliberate cross-tenant attempt was indistinguishable from the token vault being briefly down — the one event anyone would search for was the one no filter could find. The six codes the migrations raise by name are preserved now, matched on word boundaries so `INVALID_POSTCODE` is not `INVALID_POST`. **A SURVIVING MUTANT, REPORTED AS SURVIVING.** Removing `c.workspace_id = v_ws_id` from the connection lookup failed nothing, because the profile check normally does the same work — `zernio_profiles` is keyed by workspace. But `profile_id` is NOT unique, and two workspaces sharing one Zernio profile is what a shared agency account looks like; in that arrangement the profile check separates nothing and the workspace clause is the only thing between two tenants. No production workspace shares a profile today. The case is tested now and the mutant dies. Last: **a preview deployment does not run crons.** 288 `/api/cron/sweeps` hits in 24 hours on production is the measurement that Vercel's scheduler works here; four hits on `/api/cron/metrics`, all of them my own unauthorised probes, is the measurement that mine has never been scheduled — because `wt-ui-port` deploys as a preview and production is built from `wt-web`. The history starts when the branches merge, which is a founder action, not a code one.

2026-08-19 · packages/shared + apps/web (wt-design) — **The app applied a stricter readability standard to a customer's brand colour than to its own, and the token file annotated the gap with a figure nothing produces.** `brandSkinVars()` — the Readability Guard every WORKSPACE theme passes through — darkens a primary and returns `--pfg` as whichever of white or ink clears 4.5:1. Handed Sahoda's own orange it returns `var(--ink)`. `tokens.css` hardcoded `--pfg: #ffffff` on `--p: #ff6600`, which MEASURES 2.94:1 — below AA body, below AA large, and below even the 3:1 floor for a UI boundary — under a comment claiming 3.13:1, arithmetically impossible for that pair and a leftover from the #ff4b00 era. `own-medicine.test.ts` now grades the shipped file against the app's own guard, and the fix is verified on RENDERED pixels (`rgb(0,0,0)` on `rgb(255,102,0)` = 7.15:1) rather than on the token file, because a token file can say anything. The focus ring went two-tone for the same reason and a sharper one: orange misses the 3:1 indicator floor by 0.06, and the darkest same-hue orange that clears it is #fb6500 at 3.02:1 — visually identical to the brand, which would be gaming the check rather than passing it, so the ring is an ink core with an orange halo and it survives on top of an orange fill besides. **DARK WAS AN INVERSION AND THE COST WAS MEASURABLE.** Light earns hierarchy from the GAP between ink and mute (21.0 vs 7.23 — 2.90x); dark shipped #dcdcdc at 13.53 against 18.56, a 1.37x gap, so secondary text was nearly as loud as primary and every card read flat. The fix was NOT to loosen the rung. A second STRUCTURAL axis (a glyph; published is a double tick, approved a single one, which is the distinction WhatsApp already teaches this audience) keeps certainty answering "how real" and lets the mark answer "what next". `STATUS_STYLES.className` was meanwhile DEAD — forty class names, reviewed and commented, none of which reach a screen. **GREYSCALE, MEASURED AS LUMINANCE RATHER THAN AS COLOUR STRINGS**, because `.is-real` and `.is-committed` share an edge and a texture and a test comparing `rgb()` would pass on hue, the one channel not allowed to be load-bearing. It also settled a thing the tokens comment only half-admitted: in DARK, `.is-committed`'s tint sits 3/1000 above the surface — the hairline is doing 100% of the work, and a tint can never be a signature on its own. **THE SIBLING-SHAPE DEFECT, TWICE IN ONE SESSION.** At 390 with a workspace the topbar ran to 407px and the user menu sat off-screen; the @smoke guard was green because it signs in and NEVER bootstraps a workspace, so it measured a row three items short — the credit pill only exists once a workspace does. Collapsing the switcher at `narrow` (700) fixed the phone and left 768 broken by 91px, because the command palette appears at 700 and the row gains an item exactly where the rail has not yet given width back. Fixed at `wide` (1180), where the RAIL collapses. The guard now sweeps six widths x two account states. **TWO THINGS NEARLY WRITTEN UP AS BUGS AND ARE NOT**: the black "N" in every dev screenshot is `<nextjs-portal>` (confirmed by `elementFromPoint`), and a full-page capture renders `position:fixed` chrome at its scroll offset, which makes the mobile bottom bar look inlined halfway down the document. Reading `innerText` to judge visual width is a third — it returns the whole string through a CSS ellipsis, so the switcher looked untruncated when it was truncating correctly. **AND THE GALLERY'S OWN GUARD CAUGHT ME**: a disabled Tile labelled "Coming soon" made `getByRole('button', {name:/coming soon/i})` match, which is precisely the anti-pattern the rule forbids — `disabled` means temporarily unavailable, unbuilt is a `<span>`. Passing `onRemove` to a Chip from the server-rendered gallery returned a 500; an interactive primitive is a client component AND its handler must originate in one. Both kept as documentation rather than quietly fixed.

THE PREDICTION IS NOT THE MEASUREMENT. §5.3 was marked UNRUN and honest about it; running the unmodified spec on `wt-camp + wt-composer` first cost 6.7m and turned "will fail" into `waiting for getByRole('button', { name: /^continue/i })`, twice, with the page snapshot showing both channel tiles `[pressed]` and a `textbox "Your post"` where the button should have been — which is also what proved the redirect itself was fine. The Finished click is followed by `waitForURL`; the All click never was, so the assertion could be made against the still-filtered page and spend all 15s of its retries on a list that was correctly empty. It passed every dev-server run and failed once on a production server, where a click and the assertion after it land closer together than a dev server ever let them. Both clicks now wait. 2026-08-20 · packages/publishing + packages/shared + apps/web + apps/jobs (wt-zernio) — **The dry-run validator was called for the first time, and its most important answer is that a `valid: true` from it is worth nothing on its own.** `POST /v1/tools/validate/post` SILENTLY SKIPS a platform name it does not recognise: `x` and `gbp` — the two strings this product's own code uses — return `valid: true` on a 200,000-character body, while `twitter` and `googlebusiness` return the error. docs/31 §4 predicted exactly that asymmetry and asked it be tested before any validate call shipped; it is real. It also **fetches media for Instagram and for nothing else** — a URL that 404s passes on linkedin, googlebusiness, twitter and pinterest — so of the six Constraint Engine bounds docs/31 §6.3 hoped one call would settle, it settled ONE: `instagram.imageDims.aspectRange` is `[0.75, 1.91]`, measured at the boundary (750×1000 accepted, 749×1000 refused), and our floor of 0.8 was wrong in the direction that refuses a customer's legal photo. That is true of separately-authored segments and false of a SPLIT: a thread is now the one body cut at the per-segment limit, so every segment is a substring of `publishedTextOf(formatForPlatform(...))` — the exact string the gate already checked — and a red line in the last post was in front of the classifier all along. The covering property is asserted over five bodies and four limits, and asserting it rather than assuming it found **two real defects in the splitter that no "does every segment fit" check could ever catch, because both produced segments one character SHORTER**: the sentence breakpoint matched the full stop AND the space after it and cut at `match.index`, so every terminal `.`, `!` and `?` was deleted; and `RegExpExecArray.index` is a UTF-16 offset while the splitter slices CODE POINTS, so every astral character before a break shifted the cut one place right and "…family 👨‍👩‍👧‍👦 and more text…" published as "…and mor text…". **THE SECOND BLOCKER — X's 280 is per segment while `validateVariant` measures the whole — was answered by swapping ONE violation code and not the check.** `runPublishPost` filters `MAX_CHARS` out for a thread and leaves `MAX_HASHTAGS`, `MAX_MEDIA_COUNT` and `MEDIA_REQUIRED` standing; skipping `validateVariant` wholesale would have silently widened a guard while appearing to narrow it. The swap is conditioned on the CHANNEL really offering the format, so a stored LinkedIn row claiming `thread` — which the DB CHECK permits — does not buy a free pass on 3,000 characters. **A PARAMETER TWO CALLERS SOURCE DIFFERENTLY IS A DISAGREEMENT WAITING TO HAPPEN.** `planThread` took a `hasLink` boolean; apps/web sets it from `detect-link` and apps/jobs NEVER sets it — `store.ts` declines to copy a 300-line TLD list across the package boundary and says so in a comment. The flag is gone; both sides derive it from the same string, and the test sets `draft.hasLink` to true, false and undefined and requires the split to be identical. **THE API SPEAKS FOUR PLATFORM VOCABULARIES AND NO TWO AGREE**, read out of each endpoint's own 400 by calling it with an ObjectId that cannot exist: publish takes `x` and `google`; validate takes `twitter` and `googlebusiness`; `/posts/{id}/edit` takes _"twitter|discord|facebook|reddit"_ and nothing else, so **exactly one of our four channels can have a live post edited**; `/unpublish` takes `googlebusiness` and REFUSES `google`, and omits Instagram entirely. Reusing `ZERNIO_PLATFORM_NAME` for recovery is the obvious tidy-up and silently breaks Google — there is now a test asserting the two maps DISAGREE. **WHERE ZERNIO CHECKS NOTHING, WE MUST.** Its validator enforces polls completely (2–4 options, X answers 1–25 chars, duration an INTEGER 5–10080, LinkedIn question ≤140 and four duration codes, both mutually exclusive with media and with threads — every bound tested one step either side) and enforces Google's `platformSpecificData` **not at all**: `topicType: 'BANANA'` passes, and so does a `callToAction` with no `url` that their own schema marks required. 2026-08-19 · apps/web (wt-signal) — **The honest place for a fact is the altitude at which it is true, and the Signal Resolution Console nearly shipped one a rung too low.** The console's whole job is to show what Sahoda guessed and where the guess came from, so the obvious build stamps a source under every field — `FieldMeta.source` already accepts a free string, and the door label (`acme.com`, `menu.pdf`) is right there at resolve time. The remedy was the same one doc 27 reached for the dash — delete the slot. Origin is now stated once, in the header, from `brand_memory.source`, a real column `readBrain` had always SELECTed and always discarded; and the ABSENCE of per-field evidence is stated outright as copy, because a console that shows a guess and says nothing about its source invites the reader to assume there is one and it is merely unshown. What replaced the fake evidence is better than it and was already in the data: `FieldKindSchema` says of `asked` "only they know it. NEVER guessed", eleven of the fifteen registered fields are `asked`, and a resolve fills all fifteen — so a fresh brain holds eleven model answers to questions the contract forbids the model to answer. Verified by running `nextFieldMeta` directly rather than by reading it. The fix was not better wording but a narrower control: emptying is offered on OPEN LISTS only, where "there are none" is a position a person can hold, and never on a text field, where a blank `core_promise` is an absence rather than an answer and recording "a person confirmed this" over nothing would put a hollow field into the confirmed count — the exact fake-confirmation state `field_meta` exists to prevent, arriving through the one control that can empty a field. 2026-08-19 · apps/web (wt-signal) — **A port is not yours because you used it; four sessions share one machine and the failure mode is testing someone else's build under your own name.** `playwright.config.ts` defaults `E2E_PORT` to 3100, and 3100 was already a peer session's dev server — so the first smoke run reused it via `reuseExistingServer` and would have reported that session's code as this lane's result. Checking the OWNER of a listening port, not merely that something answers, is the only thing that distinguishes "my build is up" from "a build is up". The same run produced three separate environmental failure signatures that all LOOK like application bugs and are not: `TimeoutError: page.waitForURL` on the sign-in fixture ×13, whose real cause appears once as `getaddrinfo EAI_AGAIN leading-hyena-7.clerk.accounts.dev` — transient DNS, since the same lookup succeeded minutes later; `net::ERR_CONNECTION_REFUSED` ×10 from the server dying mid-suite; and a `pnpm dev` webServer that never finished compiling inside its 180s budget under four concurrent sessions, which fails as a BLANK WHITE screenshot rather than as an error. The last one is worth naming because a blank frame reads as a rendering bug in whatever page you happen to be working on. Running the suite against `next start` on an already-built tree removes that arm entirely — the pages are compiled, so a slow machine costs seconds rather than the whole budget. Also: `@sahoda/shared` cannot be imported from an e2e spec at all. It reaches `pricing.config.json` and Playwright's ESM loader rejects it with `needs an import attribute of "type: json"`, which surfaces as `No tests found` — a collection failure that reads exactly like a bad `--grep`.
